# Chapter 3 Partnership and collaboration in the pharmaceutical industry

#### Abstract

A significant contributor to the creation of treatments and vaccines is the pharmaceutical industry. To develop vaccines or treatments (like COVID-19), relationships and open, cross-disciplinary collaboration among scientific and research communities, startups, and pharmaceutical firms have been critical success factors. The chapter looks at the evolving models of open innovation (OI) that pharmaceutical companies created, funded, and supported to interact with startups to accomplish sustainable corporate growth. The foundation of the open innovation model is the idea of an alliance between an established business and a startup. The public-private partnership (PPP) system is a startup-company asset-sharing arrangement that can be used to foster innovation in a variety of drug discovery and development contexts. The chapter primarily focuses on the various types of collaborations and their significance in the pharmaceutical industry. The conclusions of the chapter show that the pharmaceutical industry needs to work with startups, producers, and businesses from a variety of fields to collaborate and accelerate innovations when new pharmacological mechanisms, enabling techniques, and translational models are discovered.

Keywords:  partnership,  collaborations,  pharmaceutical industries,  public-private partnership,  start-ups,  challenges,  

## 3.1 Introduction

It is unambiguous that the pharmaceutical industry needs to supplement its traditional approach to innovation with a quick approach that focuses on getting acquainted with startups and innovators quickly and repurposing concepts, expertise, and technologies that are easily accessible. The key success factors in developing vaccines and discovering treatments (such as COVID-19) have been the relationships and open, cross-disciplinary collaboration among the scientific and research community, startups, and pharmaceutical companies. The role of corporate venture funds, partnership initiatives, and internal and external systems for sourcing and scouting was never as crucial in generating ideas that pharmaceutical companies could adopt or in coming up with novel approaches to employing already-existing facilities, services, and products as coronavirus crisis solutions. Large companies’ main strategic reasons to participate in open innovation are to forge new alliances, investigate emerging technologies, and spot new business prospects \[[1](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_001), [2](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_002)\]. Academic, commercial, and nonprofit partnerships can give projects with low financial returns enough momentum to advance; this is especially true for the drug discovery industry. Public-private partnerships (PPPs) offer strategic guidance; industry groups are dominated by strongly product-focused processes and wide expertise; academics typically collaborate on high-risk projects alongside little financial return; and each partner brings a unique set of skills and technical know-how to the table that benefits the project as a whole \[[3](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_003)\]. The need to sustain and grow shareholder value and successful manufacturing levels drives the pharmaceutical industry continuously. When it comes to advancing candidates through the development pipeline, small and medium-sized pharmaceutical and biotechnology businesses with stronger ties between research and development have outperformed their larger sisters. To address unmet market needs, they have become more adept at creating biological products, including monoclonal antibodies, vaccines, and peptides \[[4](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_004), [5](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_005)\].

PPPs are partnerships between private businesses and public organizations that are intended to carry out specific projects. These contracts are usually used to fund initiatives like building parks and public transport systems. Within the pharmaceutical sector, these agreements facilitate the funding of research and development (R&D) endeavors via public funds or charitable organizations to create novel drugs, particularly in therapeutic domains with unfulfilled medical needs and lower levels of pharmaceutical research activity \[[6](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_006)\]. The drug industry’s wide expertise and highly product-focused processes, combined with PPP strategic guidance and academics’ propensity to work on high-risk, low-reward projects, can create a fertile and productive environment \[[7](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_007)\]. Partnerships, alliances, and collaborations can lower overall costs and lengthen development times while significantly increasing clinical success. Through them, every business is limited to maintaining its fundamental skills and is dependent on outside parties to learn about its areas of non-competence. R&D executives have realized in recent years that internal efforts alone are insufficient to sustain the entire scope and depth of research projects. Numerous pharmaceutical companies have moved their R&D centers close to some of the top universities \[[8](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_008)\]. National Cancer Institute–Technology Transfer Center (NCI-TTC) facilitates partnerships and collaborations for licensing with businesses of all sizes, across a wide range of geographies and development stages, and with a variety of life sciences research and development interests. Leading healthcare corporations, possessing ample financial resources to conduct clinical trials, are essential for introducing innovative technologies to the market. Large pharmaceutical companies, along with small and early-stage companies, must thus collaborate and license partnerships because they have an increased need to have access to expert clinical and research expertise, hands-on experience, and new technologies \[[9](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_009), [10](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_010)\]. Researchers examined the efficacy of industry-university collaboration as a comprehensive endeavor with a variety of stakeholders and goals. Organizationally, one of the most important things from the company’s point of view is the firm’s capacity to take on new technology and incorporate it into the business value chain. Scholars bolster the idea by asserting that businesses prioritize the impact of research over project results. An important criterion for assessing the efficacy of knowledge transfer is how the new information will improve an organization’s performance. Strong leadership, senior management support, incentive structures, and researcher motivation are all significant university-level factors that impact collaboration \[[11](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_011), [12](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_012), [13](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_013)\].

Research and development cooperation between universities and the pharmaceutical sector is not a new idea in and of itself. It was established some time ago to promote the development of new products and maintain pharmaceutical innovation \[[14](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_014), [15](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_015)\]. Technology and knowledge transfer from academia to business through licensing has increased in frequency. However, the industry and the university have different objectives. Conversely, researchers plan to publish their findings in specialized journals to disseminate their findings to the general public. Academic reputation is more significant to researchers than profit, but industrialists place a great deal of importance on financial performance \[[15](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_015), [16](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_016), [17](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_017)\]. Moreover, although universities lead the way in scientific innovation, most fields see product- or process-oriented technical change taking place within businesses. To achieve sustainable corporate growth, the work investigates university-pharmaceutical industry cooperation in Portugal’s Centro region. The discussion will center on whether the seemingly at-odds interests work to the advantage of pharmaceutical companies \[[18](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_018), [19](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_019), [20](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_020)\]. It has developed into multi-stakeholder, more horizontal PPPs. One of the primary reasons is that the multi-stakeholder PPP model establishes the essential commonality that enables government agencies, educational institutions, patient organizations, health foundations, and business organizations to aggregate resources and expertise. PPPs enable these stakeholders to collaborate to resolve the primary concerns that are of mutual interest in the pharmaceutical innovation sector \[[21](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_021), [22](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_022)\]. In recent times and with ever-increasing intensity, Big Pharma has been an important participant in transactions like collaborations and partnerships, mergers and acquisitions (M&A), outsourcing of products and procedures, and others that require a complete reorganization of the R&D. Pharmaceutical industry multinationals are starting to realize the full potential of open innovation, emulating other sectors that were early adopters of knowledge sharing. Furthermore, the FDA and EMA decided to open up their clinical trial database to anybody, enabling them to use the data to speed up the development of new medications and simplify the approval process \[[23](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_023)\].

## 3.2 Types of partnerships involved in pharmaceutical industry

When more than one of these organizations collaborates, some of the best work toward the discovery of novel medications is produced. The drug industry’s highly product-focused processes, broad expertise, inter-firm PPPs’ strategic guidance, and academics’ propensity to work on high-risk, low-reward projects can create a fertile and successful environment. These kinds of partnerships have surfaced as shown in [Fig. 3.1](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_fig_001).

![Fig. 3.1: Various types of partnerships in the pharmaceutical industry.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-003_fig_001.png)

Fig. 3.1: Various types of partnerships in the pharmaceutical industry.

### 3.2.1 Inter-firm collaborations

Although a lot of research has been done on venture capital contracts, less has been written about the difficulties that arise in inter-firm R&D partnerships, like those found in the pharmaceutical sector, where intellectual capital plays a major role. There are more reasons to research these inter-firm collaborations because it has been noted that patents and intellectual property rights are essential to the financial success of any pharmaceutical company \[[24](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_024)\]. In the pharmaceutical sector, inter-firm contracts are not uncommon; however, there is a notable variation in the terms of these contracts. Every contract partner may find different terms more or less appealing. Startup businesses typically prefer upfront payments, but large pharmaceutical companies may view them as risky if drug development fails. Large pharmaceutical companies usually prefer royalties as a profit-sharing mechanism after a drug is successfully commercialized because they lower the risk of initial payments \[[25](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_025)\]. Although the pharmaceutical sector offers an abundant setting for examining inter-firm strategic alliances involving intellectual capital, these kinds of partnerships are not exclusive to the healthcare sector. Other industries, especially those involving cutting-edge technology, have inter-firm alliances where businesses collaborate to share intellectual property and other complementary skills and resources to support successful product development and commercialization \[[26](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_026)\].

### 3.2.2 Public-private partnerships

Such initiatives, which vary from two-party agreements to large, both privately and publicly financed consortia, involve companies, colleges, universities, governments, and nonprofit and nongovernmental organizations within the life science sector. Open access and controlled collaborations can facilitate sharing, which can be highly beneficial for both presently under-researched and under-focused areas of study. It might create avenues for novel discoveries, which would then benefit public health. While the pharmaceutical industry offers numerous asset-sharing models, the PPP model has garnered attention as a potent means of stimulating innovation across the spectrum of drug discovery and development. To further research in specific disease and therapeutic areas, or to achieve specific objectives, such as target identification, target confirmation, biomarker growth, and drug safety and efficacy evaluation, PPPs have also been introduced. PPPs that are specific to certain diseases or therapeutic areas include the Alzheimer’s Disease Neuroimaging Initiative (ADNI), Dundee Kinase Consortium, Accelerating Medicines Partnership (AMP), National Cancer Institute (NCI) Community Cancer Centers Program (NCCP), and Coalition against Major Diseases (CAMD) \[[27](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_027), [28](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_028), [29](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_029), [30](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_030), [31](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_031)\]. Furthermore, pharmaceutical companies have developed publicly accessible screening libraries, such as the Tres Cantos Antimalarial Set (TCAMS), which was assembled by GlaxoSmithKline (GSK). Numerous individuals within the malaria community have successfully implemented TCAMS \[[32](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_032), [33](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_033), [34](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_034), [35](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_035), [36](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_036), [37](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_037)\]. The Structural Genomics Consortium (SGC), a nonprofit PPP that was established to identify protein structures that may serve as targets for drug discovery initiatives, receives the backing of numerous pharmaceutical companies \[[38](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_038)\].

### 3.2.3 Academic-industry partnership

Academic medical centers are investing resources to guarantee successful collaborations with pharmaceutical companies, as they have rekindled interest in doing so. These collaborations shouldn’t be one-sided, with information flowing both ways. Their pharmacological counterparts can provide academic colleagues with valuable insights. The majority of academic centers could benefit from the exchange of experiences regarding technologies, knowledge management, project management, and operational excellence. Pharma must establish a method to provide academicians with access to its extensive chemical libraries, confidential data sets, cutting-edge tools, and technological platforms. It is essential to participate in strategic, cooperative innovation in biomedicine to fully leverage the potential of novel biomarkers or disease targets and to stay up-to-date with the rapid tempo of discovery. Pharma’s present business strategies are predicated on the identification of clinical candidates through acquisitions, partnerships, and mergers. It is essential to forge active partnerships with academia to address critical scientific inquiries and convert novel targets into therapeutic programs, thereby replenishing the future drug pipeline with innovative medications \[[39](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_039), [40](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_040)\].

New translational models, enabling technologies, and therapeutic mechanisms are being discovered at an ever-increasing rate. The practice of segregating pharmaceutical research within an organization was common in the past. Similarly, academic drug discovery has changed dramatically in recent years, and working with the private sector has become more valuable. The worth goes beyond money. These multidisciplinary collaborations bring together the most innovative concepts and methods for locating new therapeutic targets, solving shortcomings in translational chemical biology, and creating new chemistry and technologies to increase the practical applications of these publicly funded discoveries \[[41](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_041), [42](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_042)\].

### 3.2.4 Collaboration with start-up

To remain competitive, expand into new markets, manage the mobility of qualified professionals, abbreviate product life cycles, and address the increasing complexity, specialization, and convergence of technologies, businesses have consistently sought new innovative approaches. The number of enterprises is increasing steadily. Startups are brimming with young talent who possess a strong sense of entrepreneurship, a willingness to take risks, a willingness to adapt to change, and the power of digitization, even though they lack resources, brand credibility, and easy access to the market. They are also awash in exciting ideas. To improve their chances of success, they typically need to collaborate with other businesses. Several organizations, including those in the pharmaceutical industry, are collaborating with startups to pursue innovation outside of their boundaries. These initiatives are well-designed and mutually beneficial, generating value for clients \[[43](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_043), [44](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_044), [45](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_045)\].

Beginning with the significance of corporate relationships and open innovation in the pharmaceutical industry as mentioned in the preceding paragraph, researchers chose to examine the developing models of OI that pharmaceutical companies developed, funded, and supported to engage with startups. To examine the phenomenon, a database was created containing the OI programs that a sample of startups had implemented \[[46](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_046), [47](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_047)\]. All businesses with billion-dollar annual sales, Italian businesses, and businesses engaged in the respiratory and central nervous system therapeutic fields were taken into account, and a rival analysis was initiated. Initiatives involving pharmaceutical companies and startups were found to be more successful than those involving non-partnership companies \[[48](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_048)\].

### 3.2.5 Collaborations with R&D industries

There are several inherent risks associated with forming a successful strategic partnership for R&D activities, particularly when considering the context of multinational enterprises’ (MNEs) knowledge misappropriation. According to resource-based theory, small and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs) nevertheless need to collaborate on R&D projects with large corporations despite the possibility of technology secrets being misappropriated. It requires strong intellectual property policies (IPPs) to protect the SMEs’ vital proprietary technology. Additionally, R&D outsourcing and collaboration are brought on by the growth of large-scale cooperative multi-technological products \[[49](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_049), [50](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_050)\]. If SMEs don’t have enough resources, big businesses won’t be able to acquire the cutting-edge information and tools needed to add value. Trust in the ability to exchange sufficient information is one of the key success factors in the collaboration between large corporations and SMEs. Based on the types of new markets that are entered, knowledge management systems, and R&D systems, researchers propose significant distinctions between open and closed innovation. Certain researchers conduct analyses of open innovation cases based on factors such as industry, size, and the types of open innovation, which include technology exploration and exploitation. According to the research, medium-sized businesses engage in more open innovation than small businesses do, and they are motivated to collaborate in the market by factors like new market development and customer needs realization. The open innovation strategy and how firm size affects the degree to which the tactic is chosen are discussed by other researchers \[[51](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_051), [52](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_052)\].

## 3.3 Motivation for alliances in the pharma sector

Small biotech companies provide the pharmaceutical industry with “external” sources of research and development. A deficiency in internal development is a primary reason for larger companies to seek new product technology from external sources. However, numerous minor factors frequently encourage the use of external procurement to facilitate the development of new technological innovations in the industry: (1) the licensee firm’s functional capabilities have a high absorptive capacity for new technologies, (2) the top management believes that external sources will complement internal sources, (3) the environment is perceived as too hostile and hazardous for R&D investment due to rapid product changes and regulatory uncertainties, (4) the company’s management is risk-averse, (5) the company has extensive experience with external sourcing, (6) the management has a strong global orientation, and (7) there are substantial opportunities for external sourcing \[[53](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_053), [54](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_054)\]. Firms can contribute substantial resources through alliances that are greater than what each individual cooperating firm could provide. These alliances frequently unite businesses that possess complementary core competencies, which facilitate the expansion of the companies into new markets, the surmounting of trade obstacles, and the development of new products \[[55](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_055)\]. The following strategic justifications for alliances are provided: (1) to resolve technological gaps; (2) to mitigate the risks associated with entering new markets, including international ones; (3) to accelerate the introduction of new products into the market; (4) to achieve scale economies; (5) to reduce trade and legal barriers; (6) to broaden the scope of current operations; and (7) to reduce divestment costs \[[56](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_056)\].

The most well-known type of relationship between for-profit and not-for-profit organizations in the healthcare context is interactions among pharmaceutical companies, healthcare providers (which include professionals and healthcare organizations), and large-scale interactions \[[57](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_057), [58](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_058), [59](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_059), [60](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_060)\]. Over most of the doctors interact with pharmaceutical companies, according to research from multiple countries, and a few percentages of US biomedical researchers accept funding from the industry \[[61](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_061), [62](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_062), [63](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_063), [64](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_064)\]. Academics, professionals, the media, and politicians are scrutinizing pharmaceutical company-healthcare provider interactions more and more, but experts are divided on whether these interactions improve social value and have positive effects \[[65](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_065), [66](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_066), [67](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_067), [68](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_068)\]. There are two categories of interactions: (a) interactions focused on education, and (b) interactions focused on research. Education-oriented interactions are those that aim to improve professionals’ (such as doctors or nurses) awareness, knowledge, and mindset toward new goods and services through the dissemination of factual and instructive information \[[69](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_069), [70](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_070), [71](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_071), [72](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_072)\]. Research-oriented interactions are those that are specifically designed to sponsor, fund, or provide assistance with research initiatives. Research indicates that pharmaceutical companies and healthcare providers that prioritize education influence physicians’ prescribing behaviors. In other words, physicians who participated in educational interactions prescribed a greater quantity of name-brand medications and administered a specific injection on a more frequent basis \[[73](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_073), [74](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_074)\]. Finally, the prescribing behavior of physicians is binary, influenced by education-oriented interactions in both investigations. In other words, the prescribing practices of physicians are not significantly affected by the increased monetary value of the education-oriented interaction \[[75](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_075)\]. Further research on research-oriented interaction reveals that doctors who identify primarily as entrepreneurs and whose professional identities are closely aligned with pharmaceutical companies are more likely to allow pharmaceutical companies to exert greater control over different aspects of research projects \[[76](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_076)\].

The corporate pharmaceutical sector is characterized by intense competition, with each organization striving to emerge as the winner in the sprint to introduce innovative blockbuster compounds. The industry knows for a fact that companies that enter the market first profit more than those that introduce follower molecules. Moreover, early adopters have enough time to refine their offerings and determine the going rate. Pharma companies are always searching for and developing new compounds that target similar or very similar drug targets \[[77](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_077)\]. These repeated research initiatives ultimately use resources that could be better allocated to the R&D of other diseases with unmet medical needs, given the enormous funding directed toward pharmaceutical R&D. Several cooperative agreements have been put forth and used to improve the performance of pharmaceutical R&D \[[78](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_078), [79](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_079)\]. Pharmaceutical companies, biotechnology firms, and academic drug discovery divisions share the goal of conducting fundamental research that will facilitate future drug discovery and innovation, even though they would otherwise compete. Consequently, they collaborate on precompetitive research. Precompetitive research, in its simplest form, determines the scientific feasibility of pursuing a specific therapeutic pathway before a full-scale research and development drug campaign is launched \[[80](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_080)\]. Precompetitive research can be conducted in several areas, such as target validation and identification, compound library sharing, and assay and biomarker development. Precompetitive cooperation has many advantages, such as lower research expenses because businesses pool resources and knowledge, increased productivity because businesses concentrate on their core skills and improve their quality, and the exchange of ideas among scientists \[[81](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_081)\]. Therefore, precompetitive studies, partnerships, and collaboration strategies have improved drug discovery efficiency. Research on rare and neglected diseases should be expanded into underserved therapeutic areas. It would protect companies with blockbuster drugs that are on the verge of expiring.

## 3.4 Challenges in pharmaceutical collaboration

Even though industry-academia cooperation is on the rise, certain problems and obstacles still affect the mechanisms of collaboration, as shown in [Fig. 3.2](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_fig_002). A distinct time horizon, confidentiality, and exclusivity; organizational variances may exist, and these can directly affect funding levels, university budgets, academic incentives, and research priorities. Industry is driven by profit-driven solutions to issues and outcomes, while university researchers are motivated by academic prestige and interest. There are certainly cultural differences between university and industrial R&D research, with the former concentrating on applied problem-solving research and the latter more exploratory. Due to divergent values and misunderstandings, it could lead to a serious conflict among academic and industrial partners \[[82](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_082), [83](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_083)\]. Collaborative and partnership research is impeded by the diversity of perspectives on knowledge production and use, as well as the connection between researcher and practitioner from top-down to bottom-up, sequential to interactive, and multidimensional \[[84](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_084), [85](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_085)\]. Based on research traditions and/or experiences, researchers and stakeholders may possess distinct fundamental assumptions regarding knowledge and learning \[[86](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_086)\]. Researchers discovered that social relationships could be weakened by role ambiguity, various positions, and role conflicts. They also identified barriers such as organizational change, cultural differences, and standards on research output, as well as positive effects on actual usage \[[87](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_087)\]. The development of the collaborative knowledge platforms that serve as the foundation for many of these innovative working practices is also beset by numerous real-world obstacles. Creating a critical mass of contributors, standardizing and ensuring the quality of the data entered, curating the databases that are produced, utilizing the right tools for analysis and visualization, and protecting clinical sample data are a few examples \[[88](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_088)\].

![Fig. 3.2: Various challenges faced by collaborations in the pharmaceutical industry.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-003_fig_002.png)

Fig. 3.2: Various challenges faced by collaborations in the pharmaceutical industry.

The absence of accountability and transparency in PPPs is an additional impediment to equitable health care on a global scale. Public inspection is frequently prohibited for partnership agreements with the private sector. The selection of private partners, the establishment of objectives to be achieved, and the development of management policies are all opaque processes. The apparent lack of transparency makes it difficult to ascertain the equity objectives that have been established and the individuals who should be held accountable for their fulfillment. Due to their intricate structures, differing processes of accountability between both the private and public sectors, and complicated governance, private companies can be challenging to hold accountable for failed PPPs \[[89](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_089)\]. The achievement of the university-industry collaboration is impacted by multiple challenges. Organizational differences present a variety of challenges as well. However, the most frequent problems in university-industry (UI) partnerships are those brought on by organizational differences between universities and industries. Industries and academic institutions have distinct institutional settings and prioritize different things \[[90](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_090)\]. The organizational structure of a university is more rigid and less adaptable than that of a business. Even though the challenges arise from these structural differences, we still don’t fully understand how various collaborations address these challenges. There is a dearth of understanding about how these organizational differences impact collaboration, particularly when students are involved \[[91](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_091), [92](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_092)\]. Another issue that affects these collaborations is an elevated level of heterogeneities, usually during the execution stage \[[90](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_090), [91](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_091)\]. The complexity of collaboration arises from the diverse characters, values, and traditions that come together to work together. Researchers frequently lament that the business isn’t giving them the information they need to carry out their work. Prior studies indicate that there are fewer organizational differences the more closely industry and academia interact. Organizational differences have an impact on the collaboration process since the parties involved in it are accustomed to seeing things from the other partner’s perspective \[[92](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_092), [93](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_093), [94](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_094), [95](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_095)\].

## 3.5 AI-assisted pharma collaborations

Partnerships between public/governmental, private/commercial, and civil society organizations can enhance the health of the impoverished by creatively integrating the diverse resources and talents of various organizations. Public agencies benefit from collaborating with the private sector in areas such as product development, manufacturing, marketing, distribution, and production process development, where the public sector is weak. Partnerships seem to be the most suitable option in the following scenarios: The impact of traditional independent methods of addressing a problem is limited. Potential collaborators can reach a consensus on the specific desired objectives. Both sectors possess relevant complementary expertise. The long-term interests of each sector are met, and the contributions of resources and expertise are fairly balanced. It is not advisable to assume that PPPs will fulfill obligations that are located elsewhere. In general, PPPs should be considered social initiatives that aim to identify more effective strategies for addressing complex health issues \[[97](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_097)\].

In response to the rapid adoption of AI in healthcare, numerous pharmaceutical companies have established joint ventures and invested in AI companies to develop more sophisticated medical instruments. These encompass the identification of pharmacological targets, the development of new medications, and the advancement of biomarkers or diagnostics. The ML/DL analyses of the underlying data are subsequently combined to develop algorithms that significantly contribute to the advancement of AI-infused modern healthcare \[[98](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_098), [99](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_099)\]. As a result, several international alliances between the pharmaceutical and artificial intelligence sectors have recently been formed. Benevolent AI is one of the AI technologies that are presently being utilized in the development of medications. It employs the text mining technique to analyze the extant patents, as well as additional genetic and biological data, to infer the connections between those entities. It leads to the development of diagrams that are highly illuminating and comprise dynamic maps with over one billion relationships \[[100](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_100)\]. Exscientia is an AI venture that focuses on the discovery of phenotypic medications. AI significantly underperforms human analysis of exceedingly intricate datasets in the realm of high-content phenotypic drug discovery. Testing each newly developed compound and subsequently comparing its anticipated performance to other molecules facilitates the rapid evolution of drug designs. Numerate is also a notable AI enterprise. Numerate underscores the potential of algorithms to revolutionize the search for new medications by addressing critical therapeutic gaps through the analysis of extensive drug development datasets, with a particular emphasis on the application of classical methods to combinatorial ML, ADMET, and ligand chemistry \[[101](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_101)\].

The development of innovative and effective remedies for a variety of diseases is contingent upon the collaboration between pharmaceutical scientists and AI researchers. They can leverage their expertise and experience to create algorithms and models that are rooted in machine learning. This will allow them to predict the efficacy of potential drug candidates and accelerate the drug discovery process. Collaboration can improve the precision and efficacy of clinical trials by utilizing AI algorithms to analyze the data acquired during these trials to identify trends and potential side effects of the medications being tested \[[102](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_102)\]. It can expedite the entire drug development process and assist pharmaceutical companies in making well-informed judgments regarding which potential drugs to pursue. Furthermore, the affordability and accessibility of healthcare can be improved through collaboration between pharmaceutical scientists and AI researchers. The efficacy of potential drug candidates for specific patient populations can be predicted by analyzing data from large populations and identifying patterns and trends. Artificial intelligence (AI) algorithms can be employed to achieve this. It enables the personalization of remedies to accommodate the unique needs of each patient \[[103](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_103)\]. The pharmaceutical industry’s advancement may be expedited by the implementation of AI algorithms and big data, according to researchers. One of the most promising applications of AI has been designated by researchers as the pharmaceutical industry’s pursuit of medications to treat uncommon ailments. Pharmaceutical companies are less inclined to allocate resources toward drug development when there is a lack of market demand \[[104](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_104), [105](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_105)\]. In contrast, AI can determine which patients would derive the most benefit from clinical trials. The risk of failure at the most expensive stage of the pharmaceutical industry, the clinical trials phase, can be significantly reduced by developing a profile of ideal candidates. Another area of intense research concentration is the role that AI algorithms will play in the displacement of humans \[[106](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_106), [107](#9783111573861_003373.xhtml#b_9783111574288-003_ref_107)\].

## 3.6 Conclusion

The pharmaceutical industry needs to work with startups, innovators, and businesses from different fields to collaborate and accelerate innovations. R&D is funded through private-public partnerships. Collaborations can save expenses, postpone development, and enhance clinical results. In the pharmaceutical sector, partnerships aid in the creation of new medications. Businesses, academic institutions, governments, and nonprofit organizations work together to share resources and intellectual property to develop and commercialize goods. Academic medical centers make partnerships with pharmaceutical companies’ investments. Both parties should exchange information in these partnerships. These collaborations provide value beyond financial gain by addressing significant scientific issues and creating new treatment targets. Working together with academic institutions is crucial to creating innovative medications when new therapeutic mechanisms, enabling technologies, and translational models are identified. Time horizons, confidentiality, exclusivity, and cultural differences between industry and university research are some of the challenges that face pharmaceutical collaboration. The lack of transparency and accountability in public-private partnerships can also impede global healthcare equity. Collaboration may be hampered by the disparities in organizational structures and heterogeneities between universities and businesses. Moreover, pharma AI, with assistance from organizations from the public, business, and civil society, works together to enhance health and tackle challenging health problems. Partnerships that have a shared objective, complementary specialties and equitable contributions are advantageous to public agencies. To create novel medications and biomarkers, pharmaceutical companies have teamed up with AI startups.
