# Chapter 6 Drug development and clinical trial via artificial intelligence

#### Abstract

Clinical trials and medication development have been profoundly impacted by artificial intelligence (AI), with deep learning techniques outperforming traditional machine learning tactics. Though its potential and accessibility are limited, high-throughput virtual screening (HTVS) has been proposed as an alternative to expensive and time-consuming methods. The chapter overviews computational drug development techniques that identify drug targets, validate drugs, and conduct clinical trials using artificial intelligence (AI). Additionally, by simply acquiring the governing equations from data, a novel deep learning technique has been described that is designed to build PK/PD models that can forecast patient response times and simulate the impact of unknown dosage regimens. The technique keeps the dose-concentration effect pharmacologically sound while enabling the model to predict treatment outcomes and replicate the patient’s dosage schedule. Drug target interactions (DTIs) are essential for locating possible drugs and offering information on side effects and drug mechanisms. Additionally, ethical issues about social gaps, security of data, informed consent, medical consultation, empathy, as well as sympathy, are raised in the last section and affect AI-enabled clinical trials in the healthcare industry.

Keywords:  drug target interactions,  PK/PD models,  high-throughput virtual screening,  validation,  ethical issues,  

## 6.1 Introduction

The application of machine learning (ML) methodologies in computer-aided drug development has increased markedly in recent years. The ability of deep learning methods, such as artificial neural networks with numerous hidden layers, to autonomously extract features from input data and model nonlinear input-output relationships has recently garnered heightened interest \[[1](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_001), [2](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_002), [3](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_003), [4](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_004), [5](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_005), [6](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_006)\]. The characteristics of deep learning methods supplement conventional machine-learning methods that use molecular descriptors created by humans. Deep learning has made a somewhat tardy comeback to the drug discovery space, but in the interim, it has yielded an unprecedented wave of novel modeling techniques and applications. The continued advances in deep learning have proven beneficial to many domains within the chemical field \[[7](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_007), [8](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_008), [9](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_009), [10](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_010)\].

Contemporary medicinal chemistry techniques primarily rely on stringent testing protocols and a cyclical methodology \[[11](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_011)\]. These methods evaluate several potential pharmacological substances to identify those possessing the requisite qualities. However, these procedures may be expensive, labor-intensive, and produce inaccurate results \[[12](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_012)\]. Their limitations may stem from the lack of appropriate test substances and the challenges associated with precisely forecasting their behavior within the body \[[13](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_013)\]. These problems can be addressed by various artificial intelligence (AI) algorithms, including reinforcement learning, evolutionary algorithms, rule-based systems, and both supervised and unsupervised learning methods. These strategies generally rely on the analysis of extensive datasets that provide many applications \[[13](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_013), [14](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_014), [15](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_015)\]. These algorithms can more precisely and effectively predict the safety and efficacy of new therapeutic molecules compared to existing methods \[[16](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_016), [17](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_017)\]. Furthermore, AI-driven algorithms can pinpoint novel targets for pharmaceutical development, encompassing particular proteins or genetic pathways linked to illnesses \[[18](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_018)\]. Enhancing drug discovery beyond conventional approaches may ultimately result in the creation of novel and more efficacious therapies. These constraints can be overcome with the implementation of ML and other AI techniques. ML algorithms can discern patterns and trends in extensive datasets that may escape human researchers. It facilitates the expedited proposal of contemporary bioactive substances with fewer adverse effects compared to traditional techniques. Recently, a dataset comprising recognized medicinal substances and their associated biological processes was utilized to train a deep learning algorithm \[[19](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_015)\]. Subsequently, the program showed remarkable precision in forecasting the action of new chemicals. Substantial contributions to mitigating the toxicity of potential therapeutic molecules have been made by utilizing extensive datasets of recognized hazardous and non-toxic substances to train ML algorithms \[[20](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_020)\]. Furthermore, the use of AI in drug discovery requires a high-quality dataset. Technology and high-throughput sequencing advances have accelerated the creation of many free, open-access drug discovery databases. As seen in [Fig. 6.1](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_fig_001), the drug discovery procedure is accelerated and assisted in entering the big data era by these databases. Presently, the manually selected databases like ChEMBL contain more than two million compounds with characteristics resembling pharmaceuticals \[[21](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_021)\].

![Fig. 6.1: Various databases for drug discovery.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-006_fig_001.png)

Fig. 6.1: Various databases for drug discovery.

ChEMBL collects data on the deposited compounds’ molecular characteristics, toxicity, target interactions, metabolism, elimination, absorption, distribution, and excretion mechanisms. ChemDB, a publicly available database, provides physicochemical properties (such as rotational bonds, molecular mass, and dissolution rate) and information on almost 5 million readily accessible small molecules \[[22](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_022)\]. Moreover, ChemDB includes several cheminformatics tools, including Reaction Predictor, MOLpro, AquaSol, and Smi2Depict that help in drug discovery. Information on genes and gene products that may interact with medications is available in the Drug-Gene Interaction Database (DGIdb) \[[23](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_023)\]. To find drug-gene interactions, users can use the search field module to enter a list of drugs or genes, or they can browse the genes in each group. Additionally, DGIdb can be automatically fetched via the web-based interface’s API. DrugBank is one easily accessible reference drug database. It currently includes many medications along with a plethora of knowledge about drug classes, interactions, target-drug relationships, and reactions. Utilizing the integrated tools, users can search, investigate, and retrieve text, images, and structural data from DrugBank. At present, it is the most extensively utilized resource globally for drug screening, layout, and metabolic prediction \[[24](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_024)\]. The two main issues with these platforms’ integration are the strict laws governing AI usage and the projected loss of jobs. However, these technologies are meant to make the task easier rather than completely replace people \[[25](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_025)\]. In addition to making hit compound identification quick and error-free, AI can also identify relevant SAR, recommend the best chemical structure, understand drug-target relationships, and suggest synthesis routes for these agents. After a basic medication is added, AI can help with quick decision-making and offer vital information for improving the drug in the right dosage form. Higher-quality products can be produced batch-to-batch more quickly and consistently as a result. Through in-depth market analysis as well as forecasting, AI can ensure the best alignment and pricing in the industry and potentially assist in determining the safety and efficacy of the agents in clinical trials.

Through the analysis of patient-relevant genome-exposome attributes, AI can assist in identifying specific patient cohorts for participation in Phase II and III clinical trials. It could enable the monitoring of the existing therapy targets of the selected participants by more advanced surveillance methods \[[26](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_026), [27](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_027)\]. The utilization of adjunct AI capabilities, such as predictive ML and alternative predictive algorithms, facilitates the preclinical evaluation of molecules and the identification of lead compounds, thereby enhancing the forecasting of lead molecules suitable for clinical trials within the designated patient population \[[27](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_027)\]. Patient dropout accounts for about one-third of clinical study failures, resulting in additional enrollment requirements that must be met for the trial to be completed, which adds to the trial’s financial and time constraints. Such issues can be avoided with close patient supervision and support in following the correct clinical trial protocol \[[28](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_028)\]. In a Phase II trial, “AiCure” has released smartphone software that tracks patients with schizophrenia who take their medications regularly. Consequently, there was a minor percentage increase in the patients’ compliance frequency, which guaranteed the clinical trial’s success.

## 6.2 Computational drug discovery techniques

Computational methods are vital and practical tools in the drug development process. Finding a chemical compound that can both geometrically and chemically fit into a particular cavity of a target protein is the aim of drug development. Computational methods have made many tasks easier, as shown in [Fig. 6.2](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_fig_002), including lead optimization, design, search, comparison, modeling, binding energy estimation, and pharmacokinetic predictions. Many advancements in the field of developing and discovering drugs have been made possible by advances in computational power and analytical algorithms and approaches \[[29](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_029)\]. High-throughput virtual screening, also known as in-silico virtual screening (HTVS), has produced a valuable alternative to the costly and time-consuming high-throughput screening experimental methods. The capability to virtually screen catalogs of compounds to enhance ligand enrichment and move forward with experimental validation has resulted in the production of many lead compounds. Large databases of virtual compounds that are either complementary with the solved receptor structure or similar to a known inhibitor (ligand-based) are screened computationally by HTVS. It enables researchers to search through big databases or compound libraries to find a narrowly targeted subset that can be used for experimental confirmation of activities \[[30](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_030)\]. But instead of requiring an exhaustive list, traditional HTS, de-novo drug design relies on methods of search to efficiently scan the chemical space. HTS can handle thousands of library members, verify binders, reproduce findings via biophysical experiments with high precision, and remove false positive hits, irrespective of the target 3D structural information. The subsequent, time- and money-efficient phase of potentially enhancing the physicochemical characteristics of hits to a lead medication depends heavily on HTVS \[[31](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_031)\].

![Fig. 6.2: Pictorial representation of computational methods of drug discovery.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-006_fig_002.png)

Fig. 6.2: Pictorial representation of computational methods of drug discovery.

In HTS, various biological effectors and modulators are evaluated and tested against distinct, particular targets. Consequently, HTS is generally favored when target knowledge is insufficient and structure-based drug design is impractical. HTS may also be utilized in conjunction with alternative tactics, including computational methods and fragment-based drug creation \[[32](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_032), [33](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_033)\]. Target discovery, molecule management, chemical preparation, assay optimization, and screening are the elements of high-throughput screening (HTS). High-throughput screening, when combined with multi-well cell-based platforms, enables the identification of small molecule modulators of associated signaling and metabolic pathways. The development of new medications for central nervous system disorders, especially neurodegenerative diseases, is fraught with risk due to the elevated failure rate of candidates after long and expensive clinical and non-clinical research. Consequently, the establishment of accurate assays for quantifying neurodegeneration is an essential initial step in the formulation of therapies for neurodegenerative diseases. The ability to pinpoint the related processes that initiate and result in cell death is essential to the development of effective assays. To this aim, assays have been developed to identify biochemical occurrences, such as energy metabolism, metabolic fluctuation, and DNA fragmentation that lead to neuronal death \[[34](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_034), [35](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_035)\]. Historically, cytoprotective studies utilizing fluorescent markers or dyes have been crucial for classifying therapies that induce neuronal death \[[35](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_035), [36](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_036), [37](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_037)\]. Pharmaceutical companies regularly employ several tests, which may be readily adapted for high-throughput screening methods, to assess the neurotoxicity of medications and their possible adverse effects. Researchers devised a high-throughput screening (HTS) method for primary neurons suitable for extensive investigations, encompassing compound libraries and whole-genome RNA interference (RNAi) \[[38, 39](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_038)\]. The method may be utilized for the screening of mammalian cell lines, including CHO cells, for minute particles \[[40](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_040), [41](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_041)\]. The selection of the readout for a cell-based experiment targeting a signaling pathway can be flexible. Target-oriented and cell-oriented screening procedures have been utilized to explore various innovative drug delivery systems for Alzheimer’s disease, Parkinson’s disease, bipolar disorder, autism, and schizophrenia. Cell-based screening concurrently assesses many targets, with the results indicating the effects on a cellular pathway or network. The models’ failure to precisely represent the pathophysiological environment of neurodegenerative diseases is a primary challenge faced by high-throughput screening in its quest for prospective therapeutic candidates. High-throughput screening is crucial to the drug discovery process for neurodegenerative diseases \[[42](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_035), [43](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_035), [44](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_037)\]. The efficacy of high-throughput screening (HTS) is constrained by both the quality and amount of the chemical library evaluated, as well as the absence of human-relevant neurodegenerative disease (NDD) models. Thus, increasing the number of tiny molecules accessible through high-throughput screening improves the likelihood of identifying effective disease-modifying therapies for neurodegenerative conditions. The advancement of cheminformatics in the pharmaceutical industry, the rise of AI in drug discovery, and the development of dynamic combinatorial chemistry (DCC) are anticipated to produce numerous compounds that could result in more targeted pharmaceuticals exhibiting efficacy in preliminary high-throughput screening (HTS) assays \[[46](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_046), [47](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_047), [48](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_048)\].

The substantial increase in the utilization of in-silico methodologies, including chemoinformatics, molecular simulation, and AI, over recent decades is rational, given the advancements in computing technology and the exponential proliferation of structural, chemical, and biological data related to a widening spectrum of therapeutic targets. Molecular docking is a preferred and effective structure-based in-silico technique for forecasting interactions between molecules and biological targets \[[49](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_049), [50](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_050)\]. The technique typically begins with predicting the molecular orientation of the ligand within the receptor, followed by assessing their complementarity by a scoring function. A ligand is positioned within a protein’s active region with matching algorithms that use chemical and shape information. Pharmacophores are used to illustrate the ligand and the protein. The distance matrix that links the pharmacophore, as well as the corresponding ligand atoms, governs new ligand conformations. For each match, the pharmacophore’s distance within the ligand as well as the protein is computed. Programs like DOCK \[[54](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_054)\], FLOG \[[55](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_055)\], LibDock \[[56](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_056)\], and SANDOCK \[[57](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_057)\] offer algorithms for matching ligand docking. A more recent approach that integrates multiple scores to evaluate the docking conformation is consensus scoring \[[58](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_058)\]. A ligand or potential binder may be approved if it exhibits good performance across multiple scoring systems. Consensus scoring usually produces notable gains in bound conformation, pose prediction, and virtual screening enrichments \[[59](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_059)\]. Nonetheless, the prediction of binding energies may still be off. Receptor flexibility continues to be a significant barrier in docking studies, especially when it comes to the movement of several crucial extra components of the receptor that are involved in ligand and catalyst binding in addition to the flexibility of the core structure. Certain techniques to address side chain flexibility are suitable and successful in specific circumstances. A popular solution for global flexibility, which makes sense from the standpoint of conformer selection, is a collection of proteins. Protein structures capable of accommodating the ligand should be included in the ensembles, as docking requires an efficient approach for acquiring and selecting reliable protein structures. Furthermore, the method’s computing expense is an additional disadvantage. The scoring function is a critical component for enhancing those merits of docking. Effective examples demonstrate the utilization of computational methods to engineer novel small compounds and evaluate results from an extensive database. However, experimental technology is still needed to achieve realistic interactions between small molecules and receptors. Docking applications may be enhanced by precise and inexpensively computed scoring functions \[[60](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_060)\].

## 6.3 AI-powered target determination and validation for clinical trials and drug development

Creating new medications is a costly and time-consuming process. The pharmaceutical industry faces challenges in maintaining a satisfactory return on investment due to exponential cost growth. Precise forecasting of clinical trial results can streamline pharmaceutical companies’ pipelines by cutting down on failure costs. It can also help representatives of investment banks and hedge funds make better decisions when managing investment portfolios \[[61](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_061), [62](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_062), [63](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_063), [64](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_064)\]. Early clinical trial outcome prediction may enhance the efficacy of pharmaceutical R&D, facilitate the creation of effective funding sources, and lead to the creation of innovative financial instruments for biotechnology research financing. Deep learning methods are quickly spreading into the field of biomedicine since they have begun to outperform humans in various tasks, such as image recognition. DL is widely applied in drug discovery and repurposing \[[65](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_065), [66](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_066), [67](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_067)\]. Pharmaceutical companies employ ML extensively in clinical trial operations; however, no validated reports have been published regarding the utilization of ML/AI predicted chances for technical as well as regulatory success methods throughout pharma R&D processes \[[68](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_068)\]. Although there are numerous methods and resources available for predicting clinical trial outcomes, the main techniques used in these methods were readouts from previous phases of clinical trials or trial design \[[69](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_069), [70](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_070), [71](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_071)\]. The goal was to integrate all scoring strategies, biological backgrounds, and multimodal data sources with the most recent deep-learning models.

Furthermore, employing a pipeline consisting of biological analytical instruments and deep learning algorithms, the in-silico team accurately forecasted the outcomes of phase I and phase II clinical trials for the first time. Interatomic, transcriptome, and pathway activity scores were employed to compute the target network scores. The scores were derived using the unique biomedical knowledge graph and the Panda Omics platform employed by in-silico Medicine. Pathway perturbation analysis and dimensionality reduction were performed using the in-silico pathway activation network decomposition analysis (iPANDA) tool. The algorithm provides a dependable approach for target identification and assessment of the synergistic effects of dual-target inhibitors and combinations, as demonstrated in [Fig. 6.3](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_fig_003) \[[72](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_072), [73](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_073)\]. Furthermore, ML is a potent subset of AI that has enhanced translational research and possesses the ability to expedite drug discovery through sophisticated data-driven decision-making solutions \[[74](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_074), [75](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_075), [76](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_076), [77](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_077), [78](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_078)\]. The utilization of ML in medication development has demonstrated significant benefits in modern times. Target identification, patient categorization, lead generation, de novo chemical synthesis, biological activity forecasting, medication repurposing, and precision medicine are included in these facets. In-silico methodologies utilizing AI and ML can enhance every stage of the drug discovery process \[[79](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_079), [80](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_080), [81](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_081)\]. The development of software and analytics platforms that integrate ML algorithms and in-silico statistical tools has made preclinical research and screening analyses much easier over the past few years. If phenotypic screening is designed and assessed based on preconceived notions about biology, it is more biased than target-based screening, but it still has some inherent bias \[[82](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_082), [83](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_083), [84](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_084), [85](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_085)\]. Virtual screenings have been a successful way to find novel therapeutic targets and identify leads for a while now, saving time and money \[[86](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_086)\]. Ultra-screens, which use high-throughput docking (HT docking) and structure-based algorithms to identify high-quality hit molecules and sample a significant portion of the available chemical space, are now a reality due to DL algorithms and in-silico advancements in hardware and computing power. As a result, there are fewer false-positive results and more high-affinity binding agents are produced \[[87](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_087), [88](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_088)\].

![Fig. 6.3: Pictorial representation of drug target identification via machine learning.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-006_fig_003.png)

Fig. 6.3: Pictorial representation of drug target identification via machine learning.

Target deconvolution and analysis of chemical abundance (MoA) can be conducted utilizing various methodologies. These technologies encompass protein microarrays, chemical proteomics, affinity chromatography, and functional genomics techniques, including CRISPR and RNA interference. A team of researchers involved in the project created a platform that can forecast drug targets \[[89](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_089)\]. It uses a Bayesian ML method that combines various forms of data collection to predict drug-binding targets. Furthermore, it used hierarchical clustering as a case study to find de novo tubulin possibilities. It also predicted targets for small compounds with unknown targets but promising clinical activity. Additionally, drug target interactions (DTIs) are helpful in the identification of potential medications, which further restricts the scope of research that can be conducted in the field of biochemical experimentation. Moreover, they may provide substantial new insights into the drug’s mechanism of action and adverse effects \[[90](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_090)\]. For this reason, DTI prediction is a necessary prerequisite to drug discovery, utilizing ML that integrates chemical and genomic space \[[91](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_091)\].

Validation of the pharmaceutical process is one of the most important and widely accepted aspects of current good manufacturing practices (cGMPs). It would appear that the quality system (QS) regulation necessitates the confirmation of the process \[[92](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_092)\]. It is the purpose of a quality system to ensure that the products being manufactured are always suitable for the functions they are designed to fulfill. The training and validation divide from the target selection and trial design approaches was applied to the meta-model to train and validate it. Retrospective validation of the human trial transition prediction \[[93](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_093)\] revealed impressive performance. Conversely, the retrospective validation method may be prone to various biases and may not correctly represent the precision of predictions produced in real-world scenarios. The majority of companies providing estimates are publicly traded small and mid-cap pharmaceutical enterprises. The study design was shaped by the proactive distribution of press releases by publicly traded small and mid-cap pharmaceutical companies concerning the results of their phase II clinical trials. As part of pipeline updates for the former, the big-cap and privately held companies, on the other hand, might either not disclose any clinical study results at all for the latter or disclose the annual discontinuation of phase II clinical programs \[[94](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_094)\]. Both of these scenarios are possible. On the other hand, when compared to large-cap pharmaceutical companies, the securities and derivatives that are available for small- and mid-cap pharmaceutical companies are significantly less liquid. Because of this, it is becoming increasingly difficult to short small and mid-cap pharmaceutical stocks. As a result, it may be necessary to make use of derivatives such as put option contracts to construct investment portfolios that are of high quality.

## 6.4 Machine learning algorithm in pharmacokinetics and pharmacodynamics

Pharmacokinetic and pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) modelings are essential methodologies in drug discovery, development, regulatory assessment, and approval. Pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic modeling is crucial for the design of clinical trials, data analysis, and dosage determination. Non-compartmental analysis, lacking structural model assumptions, produces macroscopic pharmacokinetic parameters (area, mean residence time, and variation of residence time) that define the temporal variations of concentration levels in the circulation \[[95](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_095)\]. Nonetheless, the methodology has constraints in extrapolating for applications such as including temporal pharmacodynamic data and fails to delineate the temporal evolution of pharmacokinetic data. Furthermore, blood circulation and the anatomical composition of the organs are used in physiologically based pharmacokinetic modeling (PBPK) to more precisely represent actual physiology. Nevertheless, the methodology continues to rely on structural models and requires rather extensive data for modeling. Furthermore, it is based on numerous difficult-to-validate assumptions \[[96](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_096)\]. A multitude of structure-based PK/PD models exist, each dependent on the drug’s mechanisms of action, the feasibility of doing PK/PD sampling, and additional factors. Deep learning is presently widely utilized in numerous fields, including pharmaceutical research, due to the development of increasingly robust deep learning neural networks. There has been an increasing interest in employing neural networks for modeling pharmacokinetics (PK) and pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD) \[[97](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_097)\]. The researchers demonstrated that the network proficiently captured the temporal correlations in the pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic (PK/PD) relationship by utilizing a long short-term memory (LSTM) network to assess the PK/PD data of a hypothetical drug based on an indirect response model. The LSTM network was applied to examine the data used for the evaluation of PK/PD data \[[98](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_098)\].

A novel deep learning technique creates PK/PD models that can simulate the effects of unknown dosage regimens and forecast patient response time courses by directly learning the governing equations from data. The method is unique in two respects. First, the deep learning model’s architecture guarantees the preservation of the pharmacological principle of the dose-concentration effect. This means that the model presupposes a causal relationship in which drug concentration is driven by dosage, and effect dynamics are subsequently driven by dosage. Second, the network architecture is set up so that the dosing data enters the model through two different ports, guaranteeing that the model can simulate the patient’s dosing regimen in addition to predicting the treatment data that is currently available \[[99](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_099)\]. Quantitative structure-activity relationship (QSAR) models, used to predict ADME properties and analyze complex pharmacokinetic profiles of drugs and nanoparticles, have been developed using ML techniques. Furthermore, researchers initially used support vector machines, a notable ML method, as a prospective tool in quantitative structure-activity relationship (QSAR) studies to forecast the tissue/blood partition coefficients (Kp) of organic compounds in various tissues. Subsequent research utilized ML techniques to predict many pharmacological parameters, including intestinal absorption in individuals. Researchers conducted multiple studies evaluating tissue and plasma concentrations, as well as various pharmacokinetic parameters (including AUC, Cmax, and t1/2) in both people and animals, by combining a ML-based QSAR model with a simplified PBPK model \[[100](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_100), [101](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_101)\]. Likewise, additional researchers computed the pharmacokinetic parameters, such as AUC, Cmax, Vd, and mean residence time, employing the ML/AI-based pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic modeling program BIOiSIM. BIOiSIM, an AI biosimulation program, utilizes the PBPK model, distinguished by numerous separate compartments that represent essential organs. The model was incorporated with existing in vivo datasets to forecast the first-order absorption rate constant (h1), Kp, and the blood-to-plasma ratio. The PBPK model was subsequently executed \[[101](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_101)\].

Researchers computed the interdose AUC of everolimus in recipients of renal transplants using XGBoost ML algorithms. Because the training dataset containing real patient data was smaller than that of other immunosuppressants, ML algorithms were taught on patient, simulated, and hybrid data. The estimates were subsequently compared between MAP-BE AUC estimates, derived from trapezoidal AUCs obtained from an independent database of transplant recipients with kidneys, which had been compiled through extensive sampling and a three-point limited sampling strategy utilized by the ISBA expert system. Consequently, simulated training datasets of varying sizes were evaluated using many measures, with imprecision (i.e., RMSE) as the primary parameter \[[102](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_102)\]. The best outcomes were obtained by ML algorithms that were trained on numerous simulations without patient data. However, RMSE does not represent the exact error in a specific patient; rather, it represents imprecision within the dataset. Researchers trained a PK model on a limited set of PK profiles, and during that process, they generated a MAP-BE. The model was not, however, independently validated. In a similar line, PK in whole blood and mononuclear cells in peripheral blood was explained by a different team of researchers using a PK model that did just a little bit better than the algorithm that was trained on patient data \[[103](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_103), [104](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_104)\]. Despite training on a large amount of data, the XGBoost algorithm’s performance was only slightly better than the previous MAP-BE. Because of the extremely high peak concentration at an hour, these unusual flat profiles either led to an underestimate of the atypical cases or an overestimation of the cases. The modeling approach, which intentionally anticipated a wide range of scenarios, presumably addressed these scenarios better. To enhance the ML algorithms and attain an equivalent degree of precision and accuracy for a pair of drugs, it would take a long time to gather the same amount of real patient data \[[105](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_105), [106](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_106)\]. Researchers chose to enhance ML as a test that could be used for other drugs by combining patient as well as simulated profiles in the training dataset or by relying exclusively on a high number of simulations. The last strategy turned out to be the most successful since it yielded superior outcomes over the whole AUC value range of the external validation dataset \[[107](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_107)\].

The primary difference between pharmacometrics (PM) and ML methodologies in drug development, as noted by various researchers regarding physiological plausibility and clinical relevance, is that PKPD models are grounded in both statistical analysis and fundamental biological principles; these models can vary from compartmental algorithms to comprehensive mechanistic models, such as physiology-based pharmacokinetic models. In contrast, ML is frequently, though not always, a purely data-driven approach. The biological interpretation of PM parameters can facilitate the identification of underlying mechanisms. On the other hand, ML parameters can be understood mathematically, though less so from a biological standpoint. However, some approaches to AI, like causal AI, offer more interpretability \[[108](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_108)\]. In PM, bias can also be introduced into the analysis if previous mechanistic knowledge is imputed incorrectly; however, it is not the case in ML. In PM, a model is manually constructed iteratively, with the modeler determining the next step by continuously assessing the model’s performance at each stage. It leads to a laborious and time-consuming process that may result in varying models from the same dataset by different modelers. Although ML is a more rapid and efficient approach compared to PM, it fails to adequately consider it \[[109](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_109)\]. ML algorithms encounter challenges with sparse and tiny data sets, prompting the development of PKPD modeling tools to elucidate temporal variations in biomarkers. To address the deficiency in a minimally documented RWD dataset, PKPD predictions are incorporated into a rudimentary ML model \[[110](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_110)\]. Future research may evaluate the efficacy of the hybrid method against more sophisticated ML approaches under varying degrees of data sparsity. The hybrid PKPD/ML model must be integrated into MIPD software to facilitate clinical decision-making \[[111](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_111), [112](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_112), [113](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_113), [114](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_114)\]. The hybrid PKPD/ML model, equipped with numerous predictive characteristics, must be connected with the EHR to eliminate cumbersome data entry at the point of treatment. Despite utilizing publicly available data, certain values may remain unknown in specific therapeutic settings. Utilizing imputation with standard values may be suitable for specific missing data, such as laboratory results that minimally influence model predictions. The user’s predicting uncertainty may then be amplified by the resultant uncertainty. Augmenting the training data led to diminished prediction bias and enhanced recall in the PKPD model simulations; however, it concurrently decreased accuracy on other measures. Larger synthetic data sets resulted in significantly lower effectiveness as the hybrid ML model became more like the data-generating PKPD model, requiring extensive manual tuning \[[115](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_115)\].

Nonetheless, forecasts must be continually assessed against empirical data, as ML methodologies can easily reassess the pharmacokinetic-pharmacodynamic model and its intrinsic model inaccuracies. Therefore, exercise caution when executing the plan. The PKPD model utilized for enrichment was constructed using the same data as the ML models, which is a limitation of the methodology. It suggests that specific data obtained from the PKPD parameters are redundant with other ML features. For instance, regimen-related attributes are integrated into the advanced ML models with the PKPD components, which encompass the chemotherapy regimen. The disparities in regimen feature significance between the enriched model and the base ML model exemplify the link. If the data communicated between the PKPD and the ML models were diminished, the efficacy of both the enriched and augmented models may differ \[[116](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_116)\].

## 6.5 Ethical challenges AI-enabled for clinical trials

Studies on the ethics of AI in healthcare are blatantly unbalanced, emphasizing topics other than global health, and more especially disease surveillance. There are few explanations of the ethical, legal, or practical issues needed for widespread adoption or large-scale implementation of AI-driven treatment in global health, according to a large portion of research in this area. Also, there is the interdependence of sources and data types needed for reporting and decision-support, as well as the surveillance of diseases at the individual and population levels \[[117](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_117), [118](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_118), [119](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_119)\]. Applications of AI in imaging, electronic medical records (EMR), laboratory assessment and treatment, novel drug discovery, preventive strategies, precision medicine, biological data analysis, procedural optimization, and information management for healthcare organizations have profoundly transformed the medical sector. Nevertheless, the scientific domain must confront several ethical and legal issues. It is essential to consider several parameters, as depicted in [Fig. 6.4](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_fig_004). The elements encompass social disparities, data protection and privacy, informed permission, medical consultation, empathy, and sympathy \[[120](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_120), [121](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_121), [122](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_122), [123](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_123), [124](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_124)\]. Union-based data processors or controllers manage personal information in compliance with these requirements, alongside the operations of international organizations and corporations to guarantee sufficient protection of personal data \[[125](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_125)\]. The Genetic Information Non-Discrimination Act (GINA) prohibits employers in the United States from making discriminatory decisions based on an individual’s genetic health information. AI can assess consumer health data and medical device imagery, thereby expediting health research and improving diagnosis and outcomes. Moreover, the procedure of engaging with a healthcare professional regarding ethical disclosure, decision-making ability, and documentation is referred to as informed consent \[[127](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_127)\]. Ethical duty dictates that patients are entitled to information regarding their diagnosis, health status, planned care, therapy outcomes, test results, charges, insurance plans, and other pertinent medical details. Patient consent must be unequivocal, explicit, and suitable for the intended purpose. Concerns about the issue have intensified when AI is implemented in healthcare applications.

![Fig. 6.4: Pictorial representation of ethical challenges in AI-enabled clinical trials.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-006_fig_004.png)

Fig. 6.4: Pictorial representation of ethical challenges in AI-enabled clinical trials.

Another issue that threatens societies as a result of AI development is the problem of social gaps. People in every nation on Earth face increasing levels of social inequality and a dwindling sense of social justice with every new advancement, discovery, and invention. Even though AI makes it easier to access more information about science, technology, global politics, environmental concerns, and international occurrences, it enhances socioeconomic disparity \[[128](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_128)\]. Due to the uniqueness of human emotions, it is plausible that the evolution of medical robots and humans will not occur concurrently at an accelerated pace. Medical practitioners and other healthcare professionals ought to consult with or provide counsel to their peers, in contrast to autonomous (robotic) systems. In autonomous systems, it is impractical. Conversely, it is highly improbable that patients will acquiesce to “machine-human” medical contacts instead of “human-human” interactions. The idea that medical professionals, including doctors and nurses, would provide treatment in a compassionate and nurturing setting can significantly influence patients’ healing capacity \[[129](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_129)\]. Robotic doctors and nurses are not capable of doing it. When patients interact with robotic nurses and doctors, they will no longer feel empathy, kindness, or appropriate behavior because these machines are not human. Patients also won’t be capable of behaving appropriately \[[130](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_130)\].

Similar to the way data privacy concerns the scope of such data, confidentiality deals with the appropriate handling, distribution, and storage of sensitive information \[[131](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_131)\]. Consulted sources indicate that security threats significantly affect confidentiality in the deployment of AI inside healthcare. Transferring and disseminating patient data to external parties may render it susceptible to security breaches or privacy infringements \[[132](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_132), [133](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_133), [134](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_134)\]. Furthermore, mobile devices are used in the majority of research projects on mobility disorder detection systems to gather relevant signals, transfer them to other computers for evaluation, visualize the findings, and notify users of the findings. Transparency is the capacity to understand how an AI system decides. To be more precise, the black box’s basic analytical steps and decision-making process are opaque. Algorithms that are programmed on biased data or that disregard particular demographic traits make these problems worse \[[134](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_134), [135](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_135)\]. For example, an AI system used in the United States to predict recidivism for defendants charged with crimes showed that African Americans with less serious offenses had higher risk scores than White Americans with more serious offenses \[[136](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_136)\]. Trust from the public, patients, and patients themselves will ultimately depend on transparency and the widespread application of AI in healthcare. Explainability is the next sub-issue associated with the main ethical problem, following transparency. Because ML algorithms are opaque, it can be challenging to comprehend and articulate the relationships between input data and outputs. It is related to explainability and transparency \[[137](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_137), [138](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_138)\]. Users’ trust in AI systems can be fostered or strengthened by explainability \[[139](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_139)\]. The level of confidence in the application of AI in medicine is enhanced by the accuracy of the explanations offered by the AI system. Moreover, given that AI is an essential element of healthcare and is employed in the medical sector, patient safety remains a paramount concern. The sub-issue addresses the potential harm or risks posed by AI tools or hazardous AI in the medical domain. A few benefits of incorporating AI into healthcare include improving patient safety and care quality, increasing access to healthcare, providing patients and clinicians with local real-time advice, and identifying medical emergencies like sepsis. AI-enabled support services, however, are not error-proof, and their algorithms might provide patients with risky advice or choices that could jeopardize their health \[[140](#9783111573861_008368.xhtml#b_9783111574288-006_ref_140)\].

## 6.6 Conclusion

AI has profoundly influenced medication research and clinical trials, with deep learning methodologies enhancing traditional machine learning techniques. AI can forecast the toxicity and efficacy of novel therapeutic molecules, pinpoint new targets, and formulate more potent pharmaceuticals. Premium datasets, including the ChEMBL database, ChemDB, and DGIdb, enhance drug development in the era of big data. AI can propose optimal chemical structures, drug-target interactions, synthesis pathways, and structure-activity relationships (SAR). AI can facilitate the identification of specific illness cohorts for Phase II and III clinical trials, enabling more accurate predictions of therapeutic targets. High-throughput virtual screening (HTVS) presents a cost-effective and efficient alternative to traditional approaches, although its potential and accessibility remain constrained. Methods for target deconvolution and molecular abundance analysis encompass protein microarrays, chemical proteomics, affinity chromatography, and functional genomics techniques. Deep learning methodologies have been utilized to create pharmacokinetic/pharmacodynamic models that anticipate patient response time profiles and simulate the impacts of experimental dosage regimens. Furthermore, ethical issues related to AI-driven clinical trials in healthcare include societal inequities, data privacy and security, informed consent, medical oversight, empathy, and compassion. Thus, AI can greatly enhance the optimization and integration of the formulated pharmaceutical in the suitable dosage form in the future. AI can assist in assessing the safety and efficacy of products during clinical trials prior to market introduction. However, several challenges remain before widespread adoption of the technology. Nevertheless, it is highly probable that the pharmaceutical industry will soon recognize AI as a valuable asset.
