# Chapter 7 AI-driven clinical decision support systems for pharma executives

#### Abstract

Artificial intelligence (AI) is a powerful tool that makes data processing, analysis, and interpretation possible. The following chapter offers information on the AI algorithms employed by pharmaceutical corporations in their clinical decision support systems. There are difficulties in implementing AI-CDSS, and future possibilities are also covered. AI-based algorithms such as logistic regression, support vector computations, naive Bayes, random forests, decision tree models, and ANN are expected to be critical in optimizing medication compositions and enhancing treatment outcomes, while also anticipating potential toxicity along with side effects in patient-specific dose formulation and safety evaluations. AI-powered CDSS helps doctors, nurses, and patients make informed decisions by evaluating patient data. NLP improves patient communication and electronic health records. Through meticulous analysis of the patient’s signs, symptoms, medical records, and contextual information, an NLP-driven Clinical Decision Support System can propose potential diagnoses, treatment strategies, and pharmacological interventions. These difficulties include organizational and social factors, human factors, and a lack of confidence among medical professionals.

Keywords:  clinical decision support,  pharma,  support vector machine,  artificial neural network,  decision tree,  ethical considerations,  

## 7.1 Introduction

The rising significance of artificial intelligence (AI) and machine learning (ML), along with the expanding accessibility of clinical data, has intensified interest in AI applications, namely computerized clinical decision support systems (CDSS). Initially, CDSSs were regarded as capable of supplanting doctors in decision-making processes. By analyzing significant data, a more sophisticated and contemporary understanding of the role of CDSSs is to aid physicians in making better decisions than the healthcare provider or the CDSS could accomplish separately. Contemporary Clinical Decision Support Systems (CDSSs) often offer recommendations to doctors, who are anticipated to make independent decisions and dismiss any suggestions they consider unsuitable \[[1](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_001)\]. Large multinational pharmaceutical corporations have led the industry’s vigorous use of AI, ML, and data mining technology to minimize the duration and expenses associated with medication research and development. During the business and management planning stage of drug development, artificial intelligence technologies must be strategically employed in addition to the exploratory drug discovery procedure \[[2](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_002)\]. Pharmaceutical companies wonder what kind of medications to target for their next research project, taking into account the managerial and market conditions of their business \[[3](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_003)\]. The majority of the time, choices about drugs under development may have been made qualitatively based on the opinions of the board of directors of the company or the technologies that the companies own. In other words, human judgment has historically played a larger role in drug development decision-making than formal analytic or data-based methodologies. An efficient model or approach that can raise the success rate of drug development is therefore desperately needed \[[4](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_004)\].

Artificial neural networks, decision trees, random forests, naïve Bayes, logistic regression, support vector machines, and numerous other AI methodologies have been successfully utilized in drug development. The integration of various models enhances and optimizes the drug discovery pipeline more efficiently than conventional machine learning methods. This is where collective education proves advantageous. Random forest analysis is an ensemble method for decision trees that produces individual trees by picking random samples using the bagging technique \[[5](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_005)\]. The support vector machine (SVM) is an additional tool that improves predictive accuracy by utilizing a unique approach to minimize structural risk throughout the learning process while increasing the complexity of the mathematical functions necessary for categorization. The multilayer perceptron, characterized by a complex arrangement of concealed layers, constitutes the many hidden layer architecture in deep learning neural networks. It enhances model generalization by reducing overfitting and optimizing hyper-parameter selection, resulting in superior performance \[[6](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_006)\]. Artificial neural networks (ANNs) are widely employed in drug release studies, pharmacokinetics/pharmacodynamics (PK/PD) modeling, and the simulation of biological processes. The ANN is employed to develop controlled release formulations by determining the appropriate dissolution properties, bioavailability, pharmacokinetic profile, and in vitro–in vivo correlation (IVIVC) \[[7](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_007)\]. Implantable microchips are effectively used in continuous/pulsed drug delivery methods as well as drug reservoirs for regulated drug delivery. According to reports, AI technology can provide integrated implantable robots for gene and drug delivery applications, allowing for the optimization of target-specific drug delivery. Ongoing research on magnetized microrobots for ocular drug delivery may prove beneficial for ocular targeting therapy. Nanorobot path optimization is supported by quorum sensing and bacterial foraging strategies. Bio-nanorobots are used to count the number of biomarkers associated with cancer as shown in [Fig. 7.1](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_fig_001) \[[8](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_008), [9](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_009)\]. Theranostics agent delivery along with disease monitoring is made easier with medical capsule robots (MCR), which use artificial intelligence systems in place of wireless capsule endoscopy (WCE) \[[10](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_010), [11](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_011), [12](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_012)\].

![Fig. 7.1: Various AI techniques for decision support Systems in Healthcare.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-007_fig_001.png)

Fig. 7.1: Various AI techniques for decision support Systems in Healthcare.

AI and ML algorithms are becoming more and more important in biomedical research, and they are influencing clinical decision-making processes. Consequently, they directly affect medical practice and the exchange of information between doctors and patients \[[13](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_013)\]. The foundation of the traditional doctor-patient relationship is open communication about medical advice and treatment choices, as well as personal trust. Decisions become opaque if the doctor is no longer the only one making the diagnosis or choosing the best course of treatment, but is instead heavily influenced by a machine using AI and learning algorithms. CDSS come in a variety of forms and classifications, with the ability to be delivered actively or passively and the timing of interventions among their many subcategories. It is frequently separated into categories based on knowledge and those based on non-knowledge \[[14](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_014), [15](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_015)\]. Knowledge-based systems generate rules based on patient, practice, or literature-based evidence. The system subsequently collects data to evaluate the rule and produces an output or action. Non-knowledge-based CDSS require a data source; however, rather than utilizing specialist medical knowledge for decision-making, they depend on statistical pattern recognition, ML, or AI. Despite being a quickly expanding use case for AI in medicine, CDSS is fraught with difficulties, such as those relating to data collection and comprehending the logic that the AI employs to generate recommendations (black boxes) \[[16](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_016)\].

Pharmaceutical formulations are extremely complex structures with a wide range of variables that can occasionally have poorly defined effects on how they behave. Pharmaceutical formulations are extremely difficult to characterize using conventional analytical methods due to their complex preparation technology. Hundreds of well-defined physicochemical parameters are being reduced to theoretically useful descriptions that will not influence decision support in the future. The characterization of specific difficulties in pharmaceutical technology continues to depend significantly on empirical data. Because of this, empirical modeling is the method of choice in the field to create the right model when numerical analysis of the data is used \[[17](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_017)\]. It makes it possible to build the model solely from the data, negating the need for prior knowledge and a priori assumptions. The data, which encapsulates the current understanding of the issue, is utilized to construct the model. The primary advantage of a data-driven model is its impartiality, as it is free from established theories. In the absence of well-founded theories, partially validated theories or hypotheses from other domains may lead to misconceptions \[[18](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_018)\]. ANNs exemplify an empirical modeling tool that has become highly effective for empirical modeling applications. Additionally, CDSS are employed for triage, detecting alterations in health-related symptoms, extracting patient data from medical records, providing in-patient assistance, evaluating treatments, and monitoring. As researchers and developers prepare to implement CDSS, they must consider the five “rights” of CDSS: the right information, the right person, the right intervention format, the right channel, and the right workflow time, as shown in [Fig. 7.2](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_fig_002) \[[19](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_019), [20](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_020)\].

![Fig. 7.2: Pictorial representation of the five rights of a clinical decision support system.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-007_fig_002.png)

Fig. 7.2: Pictorial representation of the five rights of a clinical decision support system.

## 7.2 Key components of AI-driven decision support system

AI-driven CDDS can aid physicians, nurses, patients, and other carers in making more informed choices. It analyzes patient data from the past, present, and future to identify faults or deficiencies in the treatment route and propose enhancements or safety issues to the user. The two types of decision support systems are ML systems and rule-based expert systems (ES) \[[21](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_021)\]. Many hospitals’ electronic health records (EHR) have been incorporated into decision support systems, which employ pre-established rules to guide decision-making. Because these systems only require binary choices, they are simpler to configure. ML systems, on the other hand, rely on statistics to make decisions. It necessitates a thorough comprehension of the standard process by which medical professionals evaluate patient data and reach important conclusions \[[22](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_022)\]. An AI system designed to diagnose *Helicobacter pylori* infection during endoscopy was shown to detect the infection with good accuracy and exploitability in a recent case-control study. These tools can assist pathologists in identifying *H. pylori* infections or take their place. When it comes to gastric cancer, AI’s diagnostic precision and efficacy are on par with or even better than those of general pathologists. Through convolutional neural networks, deep learning models can identify and classify esophageal squamous cell carcinoma and its depth of invasion in a matter of minutes or seconds. It works well for detecting adenocarcinomas as well \[[23](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_023)\]. AI models have been effectively developed to assess prognosis in patients with colorectal cancer (CRC), biliary tract cancers, and hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) \[[24](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_024)\]. The prognosis for gastric cancer is presently uncertain. Whole slide images (WSIs) were utilized to develop a deep learning model in cohort research that produced the Tumor Risk Score (TRS) as a prognostic marker. Clinical staging approaches failed to equal TRS’s capacity to uniformly categorize patients into up to five groups with markedly different prognoses, attributable to its predictive efficacy. Though they have a lot of potential, AI-driven decision support systems still need to be worked out before being applied in clinical settings. These challenges include data uniformity, effectiveness, morality, government approval, as well as medical acceptability. For gastroenterology to begin to utilize AI technology, these issues must be resolved. AI-powered decision support systems can enhance gastroenterology by offering quick and accurate diagnosis, individualized treatment regimens, and enhanced patient care \[[25](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_025)\].

The healthcare sector has experienced a significant rise in the use of AI applications, driven by the swift advancement of AI and other emerging technologies, which possess the capacity to generate new opportunities and eliminate current obstacles through effective data processing and analysis \[[26](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_026)\]. Today’s healthcare industry is better off avoiding many mistakes and unfortunate incidents due to clinical decision-making technologies and electronic health records (EHR). Examples of these technologies include those for entering pharmaceutical orders, predicting several critical diseases, and more \[[27](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_027), [28](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_028)\]. Healthcare institutions in wealthy nations are adopting AI with enthusiasm, as evidenced by a review of several real-world applications in the medical field. These facilities are utilizing AI-enabled systems to help medical staff diagnose and treat patients for a range of illnesses, as well as to maximize the effectiveness of nursing and administrative duties related to hospital services. When choosing a CDSS, there are two primary factors to take into account: the CDSS’s quality and its suitability for bridging the clinical quality gap. A CDSS’s quality must be evaluated at least on two levels: the technological platform level and the data or knowledge level utilized in its construction. Generally accepted quality assurance techniques for developing software used in the field of medicine should be used in the design, implementation, testing, and documentation of CDSSs, as they are software that may also be medical devices \[[29](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_029)\]. In the instance of CDSS built around models learned through machine learning or statistical analysis, an evaluation of the source data’s quality is required \[[30](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_030), [31](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_031)\]. Further hazards linked to AI-based CDSSs encompass bias and overfitting to the training data, among other factors. It may lead to undetected poor performance at the local location, as the CDSS cannot extrapolate from the training data. Therefore, considerable attention must be paid to alleviate any possible negative effects of CDSSs. It is crucial to carefully choose the CDSS that aligns with the site’s requirements. CDSSs necessitate rigorous acceptance testing, commissioning, and quality assurance at the local facility, similar to any other medical technology \[[32](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_032), [33](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_033)\]. ML is a discipline that examines the creation and advancement of algorithms capable of learning from and predicting outcomes based on data. These algorithms generate a model from sample inputs, enabling data-driven predictions or judgments rather than relying solely on static program instructions. A technique known as SVM constructs a hyperplane to identify outliers and analyze data for regression and classification. The technique utilizes a set of labeled training samples to create the maximum separation between two linearly distinguishable categories. The instances are depicted as points in a spatial framework, and new instances are categorized according to the side of the gap they occupy when projected into the same space. The kernel trick employs the dot product of vectors in a designated space to convert non-linear data into a linear representation. SVM is a popular substitute for AI in clinical management decision models. Nonetheless, the limitations of SVM encompass its incapacity to provide direct probability estimations and suboptimal performance when the feature count surpasses the sample size \[[34](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_034), [35](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_035), [36](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_036), [37](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_037), [38](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_038)\]. Decision tree learning is a predictive modeling technique that employs statistics, data mining, and machine learning to create a decision tree from item observations, resulting in inferences about the item’s target value. Decision trees are classified into two main types: (1) discrete decision tree models, in which the goal variable assumes only discrete values, and (2) continuous decision trees. Decision-making and execution can be clearly and visually depicted through a decision tree. Random forests, also known as random decision forests, are ensemble learning methodologies employed for classification, regression, and various other applications. They function by generating many decision trees during the training phase and producing a class that represents the average prediction (for regression) or the most frequent class (for classification) from the individual trees. Random decision forests reduce the tendency of decision trees to overfit their training datasets \[[39](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_039), [40](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_040)\]. A machine learning technique called Deep Learning Neural Network (DLNN) uses representation learning to enable systems to automatically find the necessary representations. Deep artificial neural networks and other methods are used for identifying and categorizing features from unprocessed data. DLNN may acquire intricate features from data using a hierarchical feature representation by employing several hidden layers. In contrast to networks with fewer layers, DLNNs execute input data (features) via a higher volume of mathematical operations, rendering their training more computationally intensive \[[41](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_041), [42](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_042)\]. The ANN, CNN, and DLNN models have been thoroughly analyzed as AI-based CDSS engines. Radiology is frequently employed for the diagnosis of medical conditions (e.g., breast cancer through mammography), stratification/classification (e.g., lung diseases), imaging-based prediction (e.g., CT imaging in smokers), imaging outcome prediction (e.g., pulmonary edema on V/Q scans), and multi-omics genotype-phenotype evaluation of disease behavior (e.g., multiple sclerosis). In medical imaging applications of AI, DLNN have been especially favored. These applications encompass picture segmentation, expedited image capture, lesion detection, and imaging-based clinical decision support \[[43](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_043), [44](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_044), [45](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_045)\].

Better data-driven decision-making, more efficient communication among healthcare professionals, improved patient care are all impacted by AI-based CDSS enhancement in documentation quality \[[46](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_046), [47](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_047), [48](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_048)\]. Furthermore, treating glaucoma patients presents a daily challenge for clinicians. Treatment of glaucoma requires the synthesis of data from multiple visits over an extended period from various sources (tonometry, pachymetry, perimetry, optical coherence tomography disc pictures, ocular exams, patient history, and so on) \[[49](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_049), [50](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_050)\]. These data have a high test-retest variability and are frequently complex. The patient’s unique circumstances must be taken into consideration when interpreting the data. An additional challenge is that, in the middle of a busy glaucoma clinic, decisions must be made swiftly based on the evaluation and integration of the data. Certain challenges may be addressed by recent developments in the area of predictive modeling \[[51](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_051), [52](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_052)\]. Predictive models for glaucoma must deliver clinically relevant information to decision-makers at the optimal time within the clinical workflow to enable informed judgments that substantially improve clinical practice. Clinical decision support (CDS) technology can aid in mitigating these challenges \[[53](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_053), [54](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_054)\]. Contemporary Clinical Decision Support (CDS) systems, which are computer-based instruments, are intended to aid clinicians in enhancing decision-making for specific patients \[[55](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_055)\]. These devices have successfully addressed various non-ocular conditions, including diabetes, cancer, sepsis, acute respiratory distress syndrome, hyperglycemia, and hyperbilirubinemia in neonates \[[56](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_056)\]. The use of diagnostic tests and treatment choices can both be enhanced by CDS systems \[[57](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_057), [58](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_058), [59](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_059)\]. An example of an effective CDS system is an add-on app for an electronic health record (EHR) that manages neonatal bilirubin levels.

## 7.3 Integrating AI into pharma executive decision-making

The increasing usage of AI-based applications has given rise to worries regarding data security and privacy. Health information is often the target of data breaches due to its sensitivity. Thus, patient data security is crucial \[[60](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_060)\]. Legal actions have been initiated due to data sharing between major healthcare institutions and AI companies, raising concerns among patients regarding potential breaches of their privacy throughout the data collection process. After data acquisition, the ensuing challenge is the advancement of AI technology \[[61](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_061)\]. Overfitting may arise when a system identifies spurious associations between patient attributes and outcomes. It results from having an excessive number of variable parameters compared to outcomes; consequently, the algorithm predicts using unsuitable features \[[62](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_062)\]. Recent years have seen a significant increase in interest in the application of AI in the pharmaceutical industry and clinical healthcare, which has resulted in a growing corpus of relevant literature and research. It offers information on how AI can improve clinical results and patient care, which is consistent with healthcare management and personalized medicine \[[63](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_063)\]. Big data practices consider upcoming talent requirements and fresh applicants in the healthcare industry. Big data presents a significant opportunity for medical practitioners, pharmacy personnel, and health policy specialists to make data-informed decisions that will eventually improve patient care \[[64](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_064), [65](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_065)\]. It has the power to alter how healthcare HR practices are ingrained and equipped to boost productivity across the board, gain a deeper understanding of data repositories, and reach a stated opinion or choice. Big data has been essential to any human resources department in the following situation, not only for keeping employees but also for their ability to better understand their surroundings and make the best decisions \[[66](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_066), [67](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_067)\]. Three strategies can be used in a big data-driven decision-making process as shown in [Fig. 7.3](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_fig_003) \[[68](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_068), [69](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_069)\].

![Fig. 7.3: Three strategies used in a big data-driven decision-making process.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-007_fig_003.png)

Fig. 7.3: Three strategies used in a big data-driven decision-making process.

Researchers have proposed utilizing ANNs’ ability to reveal concealed information and values to enhance care efficiency and support the healthcare sector in adopting “lean thinking” or value-based decision-making \[[70](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_070)\]. An example of how ANNs aid in the incorporation of Lean thinking in healthcare is through the modeling of “information flow” among cancer patients, demonstrating the relationship between quality of life and clinical outcomes \[[71](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_071), [72](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_072)\]. Support vector machines are considered advanced methodologies for addressing issues that conventional statistical analysis cannot answer. They are utilized for modeling high-dimensional data. Despite their analytical capabilities, broad implementation remains challenging, mostly due to methodological complexities and scalability concerns \[[73](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_073)\]. A systematic examination of deep learning models employing data from electronic health records identified issues associated with information inconsistency and timeliness, including concealed correlations among clinical features arising from both short-term and long-term events. The models’ performance may decline if these difficulties are not sufficiently addressed \[[74](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_074)\]. Research demonstrates that ANNs are extensively utilized in business applications, especially in decision assistance, stock price prediction, and financial distress and bankruptcy matters. Furthermore, hybrid networks have been developed to improve the efficacy of traditional models in commercial environments. The integration of ANNs with secondary AI and meta-heuristic approaches, including fuzzy logic, genetic algorithms, bee colony optimization, and artificial immune systems, has been suggested to address challenges related to ANNs, such as network structure selection, initial weight assignment, and control parameter optimization. Hybrid intelligent systems are employed in various fields, including natural language processing, robotics, medical evaluation, and manufacturing process oversight \[[75](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_075)\].

The efficacy of AI-CDSS deployment is influenced not only by technological progress but also by human issues; including explainability, privacy, and equity, together with the social and organizational dimensions of medical practice \[[76](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_076), [77](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_077), [78](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_078), [79](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_079), [80](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_080)\]. For example, because end-user clinicians in the following case do not trust or comprehend AI-CDSS’s capabilities, they may be reluctant to adopt or use it. One reason could be that many AI-CDSS operate like “black boxes,” meaning that clinicians are unable to understand how they come up with the recommendations. These problems might result in low system uptake and user acceptance \[[81](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_081), [82](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_082)\]. Researchers involved in the project assert that the success of a cooperative alliance between human decision-makers and AI-integrated systems heavily relies on the trust that users have in these systems. It indicates several variables influencing confidence in CDS systems \[[83](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_083)\]. For instance, the two sources from which the participants sought validation of medication recommendations were the guidelines published by professional societies and the articles published in reputable clinical journals. Although guidelines are regarded as more reliable recommendations, physicians are aware that guidelines change more slowly than findings from publications. Fundamentally, faith is placed in the underlying processes of clinical research, particularly randomized clinical trials \[[84](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_084)\]. The randomized clinical trial method has long been subject to criticism due to its purportedly tenuous connection to the actual medical experience and is not without controversy \[[85](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_085)\]. It has been demonstrated that participants in clinical trials tend to be younger, sicker, and more male than those who would use the experimental treatment or medication in question.

Validating and putting AI to use in medicine are still major tasks that need to be completed. AI technology currently requires significant innovation in governance and regulatory oversight before it can be widely used in a safe and morally responsible manner. It is imperative that AI be validated in clinical settings and that algorithms be published in peer-reviewed journals \[[86](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_086)\]. Perhaps in the future, AI should collaborate with scientists and medical professionals to give patients the best care possible rather than trying to take the place of specialists. AI is unlikely to handle all aspects of patient admission, diagnosis, and treatment selection. Therefore, it is imperative to attend to a patient’s spiritual and social needs as well. It is doubtful that machines will be able to provide these kinds of care \[[87](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_087)\]. For any algorithm to be validated in a clinical setting, some training is necessary. Patient data must therefore be exchanged between healthcare facilities and occasionally across international borders \[[88](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_088)\].

## 7.4 Regulatory compliance and ethical consideration

The pharmaceutical regulatory affairs sector may experience a comprehensive revolution with the application of AI, notwithstanding its present nascent adoption in the domain. AI can assist in regulatory matters by providing current knowledge on the newest developments in the sector. It is particularly accurate, considering its potential benefits for drug discovery and regulatory approval. AI can expedite and enhance decision-making for professionals in drug discovery by identifying novel prospects, automating tasks, and augmenting decision-making capabilities. Their ability to expedite the introduction of new medications is enhanced as a consequence. The term “regulatory affairs” refers to the divisions or units inside pharmaceutical companies that act as a point of contact for national and international regulatory bodies whose job is to grant product approval by current rules and regulations. Regulatory bodies, on their part, grant product approval based on data and records submitted during drug discovery and product development \[[89](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_089), [90](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_090)\]. Examples of consumer technology advancements with applications in the healthcare industry include blockchain, internet-based computing, 3-D bioprinting, and AI. In light of these advancements, new regulations are necessary to keep up with the quickly evolving field of technology \[[91](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_091)\]. The US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) is a primary catalyst for the advancement of these breakthrough technologies, establishing new objectives to enhance the regulatory approval process and offering technical standards and recommendations. The extensive uses and recent progress in AI algorithms indicate that medical technology and healthcare will be profoundly affected. Artificial general intelligence (AGI) denotes artificial intelligence that can do any cognitive activity achievable by a human, demonstrating intelligence akin to that of a human being. Artificial narrow intelligence (ANI), a type of AI commonly utilized in medical devices, is defined as AI engineered for a specified purpose, as per an FDA assessment \[[92](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_092), [93](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_093)\].

The GDPR establishes a new legal framework for scientific research that profoundly affects the rights of data subjects. Union or Member State legislation may permit exceptions to the rights of access (Article 15), rectification (Article 16), erasure (Article 17), restriction of processing (Article 18), and objection (Article 21) when personal data is used for research and development purposes. These exceptions are granted only if the fulfillment of the research objectives would be impossible or would be materially hampered by the rights; otherwise, no exceptions will be granted \[[94](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_094)\]. The GDPR does not offer complete protection against AI systems since it solely addresses two aspects of AI system regulation: the processing of private information and shielding data subjects from ADM. In the domains of healthcare services and medical research, the regulation of these entities requires a more complex legal framework accompanied by rigorous enforcement \[[95](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_095)\]. The European Commission (EC) underscored the necessity for a regulatory framework to facilitate the new technology while managing its associated dangers, notwithstanding the absence of a distinct legal structure for AI within the EU to date (European Commission, 2020). The ramifications of AI technology for the economy, legal framework, and society have rendered it a significant policy issue for the European Union in recent years \[[96](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_096)\].

All relevant players must comply with and enforce the same ethical standards and principles governing the development and application of AI technology in the healthcare sector. Artificial intelligence technology utilizes many data sets and techniques, including supervised, semi-supervised, and unsupervised learning. Health practitioners and academics exercise caution regarding these “machine-driven” analytical techniques, despite their potential, due to their intricacy. AI for Health, unlike other AI applications, directly influences human life and can have significant repercussions for patients in various aspects. The implementation of AI technologies in healthcare increases the likelihood that the system may become autonomous, hence jeopardizing human autonomy. Artificial intelligence in healthcare possesses the capacity to enable machines to make decisions. Medical decision-making and the AI-based healthcare system should be fully under human control. Under no circumstances should AI technology interfere with patient autonomy. The user or patient may not know which to believe when it comes to clinical decisions made by the AI system and the doctor because of differences in judgment \[[97](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_097)\]. The patient must be given both options in such circumstances. All research initiatives and evaluation programs must adhere to a consent protocol before the deployment of any AI technology within the healthcare sector. The advantages of employing AI technologies, along with the related social, psychological, and physical concerns, must be adequately communicated to patients. Patients must be completely empowered to accept or reject AI technologies. Moral considerations and human values must be diligently and transparently monitored at every phase of AI development and implementation.

The implementation of AI technologies in healthcare increases the likelihood of the system becoming autonomous, hence jeopardizing human autonomy. Artificial intelligence in healthcare possesses the capacity to enable machines to make decisions. Medical decision-making and AI-driven healthcare systems must remain entirely under human oversight. AI technology must not, under any circumstances, compromise patient autonomy. The patient may be uncertain about which authority to trust regarding clinical judgments made by the AI system and the physician due to these discrepancies. The patient must be presented with both alternatives in such situations. All research initiatives and evaluation programs must adhere to a consent protocol before the deployment of any AI technology within the healthcare sector. Patients must be fully acquainted with the advantages of using AI technologies as well as the risks – social, psychological, and physical – that come with using them. The ability to accept or reject AI technologies must be fully granted to patients. At every stage of the development and application of AI, moral considerations and human values should be effectively and transparently monitored \[[98](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_098)\]. The most ideal feature for any prognostic or diagnostic tool used in AI healthcare is trustworthiness. Similar to how they must develop confidence in other tools they use, clinicians must also develop confidence in AI technologies. Clinicians and healthcare providers want a straightforward and reliable method to evaluate the validity and reliability of AI technologies for optimal use. A reliable AI-driven system must deliver precise health data analysis while also being legitimate, ethical, dependable, and authentic. AI-driven technology must ensure data security and privacy during the complete development and implementation phases. Securing the confidence of all stakeholders, particularly medical receivers, in the safe and secure use of their data is essential for the effective and extensive deployment of AI \[[99](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_099)\]. The primary aim of data privacy is to avert unauthorized access, alteration, and destruction of personal information. The use of personal data by artificial intelligence must not excessively limit individuals’ perceived or actual freedom. These procedures are essential in the healthcare business, as medical information contains sensitive data that could be exploited against patients or result in prejudice, even inadvertently. Data about specific patients should ideally be anonymized unless clinical or research needs require that it be kept in an identifiable format. The duty of an individual or organization to take responsibility for their actions, account for their activities, and transparently disclose the outcomes is known as accountability. AI systems that are meant to be used in the healthcare industry need to be prepared to be examined at any time by relevant authorities. For AI technologies to operate at their best, frequent internal and external audits are required. These audit reports have to be released to the general public. The data used to train and test the system is what essentially determines the outcome of artificial intelligence, as it is a data-driven technology. It is particularly important when it comes to AI for health, as biased and skewed datasets can cause issues with bias and discrimination, among other issues. Artificial intelligence for health and other data-driven technologies are thought to be most vulnerable to data bias \[[100](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_100)\]. It is imperative to implement measures to guarantee that the “training data” is free from established biases and accurately represents a substantial segment of the intended audience.

## 7.5 Future trends of AI in the pharmaceutical industry

Artificial intelligence will usher in a transformational era for the pharmaceutical industry. Given the potential side effects and toxicity, AI algorithms are anticipated to be crucial in tailored dosage formulation and safety assessments, refining pharmaceutical compositions, and improving treatment outcomes. Wearable technologies, sensors, and artificial intelligence-enabled monitoring devices will enhance remote patient care and optimize medication adherence via tailored treatment recommendations. Artificial intelligence impacts patient selection, recruitment, and the design of clinical studies. Genetic profiles and electronic health records are employed to find qualifying patients, reduce trial costs, and accelerate approval procedures. Furthermore, it is anticipated that AI’s continuous surveillance and oversight capabilities will optimize pharmaceutical manufacturing processes, guaranteeing efficiency and uniformity \[[101](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_101)\]. As stated in certain current conceptualizations, strategic decisions are not limited to plant design or global supply chain management but rather affect all functional areas and levels of the organization. In a similar vein, decisions made in the short and medium term now span far larger domains and are not limited to production scheduling and planning tasks. Furthermore, to better meet the demands along with the dynamics of the real world, it is now imperative to integrate the various decision levels \[[102](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_102)\].

Therefore, a company’s current decision structure is far more intricate and wide-ranging, encompassing various time scales as well as enterprise functions and activities, impact and scope variations, physical network variations, and finally, multiple technological settings. In the future, the patient will be the primary influencer in determining the pertinent operational performance metrics as well as the specifications for the product or service \[[103](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_103)\]. To improve the client experience from initial discovery to therapy delivery, companies must integrate the patient’s perspective into their decision-making processes. Decision-support models will proficiently tackle challenges such as a broader range of products and volumes, patient-focused distribution networks, improved service level precision, and real-time evaluation of the whole customer experience via closed-loop information systems \[[104](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_104), [105](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_105)\]. All of the company’s technological assets are housed in the technological layer. The organization must make use of all of those capabilities to optimize the value of all assistances that can be provided with decision models that have been developed. Therefore, whenever possible, economical and/or environmentally friendly alternatives should be considered when making decisions about the most pertinent future features and particular technology selections. At the manufacturing level, production expenses, lead times, and inventory levels will inevitably diminish as more economical technologies, such as continuous manufacturing, are adopted and advanced decision-support tools, based on intricate optimization and simulation methods, are developed. Enhancements in resource and energy efficiency, along with increased flexibility and responsiveness to market variations, are anticipated to result in elevated capacity utilization \[[106](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_106), [107](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_107)\].

A multidisciplinary process should take into account the requirements of all stakeholders when developing an ML-based CDSS. This is particularly valid when these systems are integrated with XAI. Decision-makers who utilize the systems, people impacted by the decisions’ outcomes, and system designers should all be taken into account \[[108](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_108), [109](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_109)\]. It is recommended that experts in the domains of psychological and social behavioral psychology, mental health, and cognitive science collaborate when developing models \[[110](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_110)\]. Unstructured medical data continues to be a difficulty for the advancement of practical AI-based systems, despite the ability of XAI to aid in identifying data-related difficulties. It is necessary to consider domain-specific requirements, such as having a clear grasp of the system’s goal, evaluating the effectiveness and interpretability of the systems already in place, and determining the kind and volume of necessary explanations. Moreover, scientists \[[108](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_108)\] recommend against using those black-box simulations unless necessary. They recommend against using complicated algorithms that call for the post-mortem application of XAI methods whenever feasible. Rather, they suggest using transparent or interpretable by-design algorithms. The audience’s cognitive abilities and limitations, as well as implications for ethics, justice, and safety, must all be taken into account when determining the kind of explanations that should be given. More thorough user studies are required \[[111](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_111)\]. Some authors \[[112](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_112)\] claim that while giving doctors a more detailed explanation of the data used to generate the system’s recommended diagnosis boosted trust, it also led to issues with over-reliance. Less thorough explanations, on the other hand, had a reverse impact, leading participants to doubt the system’s dependability and develop problems with self-reliance \[[113](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_113)\]. Some researchers discovered through an investigation that inaccurate ML recommendations can have an impact on clinicians and reduce decision accuracy, and that explanations alone are insufficient to address an over-reliance on an algorithm that proposes incorrect decisions. It was discovered that the choice of explanation strategies should be based on the clinicians’ experience with ML and that the recommendations from ML were more useful to those who had prior experience.

However, generative chemistry, ML, and methods like Pareto optimization are increasingly being used in AI approaches for hit and lead discovery. Community-based platforms that provide resources and information for AI4DD technologies are strong supporters of the trend. Predictive toxicology is making significant strides, and regulatory bodies are positioning themselves to profit from AI \[[114](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_114)\]. AI technology can lower healthcare expenses by increasing productivity and decreasing mistakes. It is essential to acknowledge that there remain certain constraints and challenges associated with the utilization of AI in pharmacy that require resolution. For example, using AI-powered systems to track information about patients may raise security and privacy issues. Pharmacists might also require additional training to properly apply and incorporate artificial intelligence into their work. Future research may also focus on the ethical implications associated with the utilization of AI in pharmacy. As technology advances, it is essential to address concerns such as algorithmic bias, data security and confidentiality, and the possibility of AI systems supplanting human labor. By addressing these concerns judiciously and proactively, pharmacists can integrate AI in a manner that is both ethical and efficacious \[[115](#9783111573861_010289.xhtml#b_9783111574288-007_ref_115)\].

## 7.6 Conclusion

AI, ML, and data mining are being used by pharmaceutical companies more and more to improve model generalization, drug discovery, and decision support systems (DSS). There are two categories for these technologies: knowledge-based and non-knowledge-based. Empirical modeling is the preferred method for creating the right model in pharmaceutical formulations, eliminating the need for prior knowledge and assumptions. AI-powered CDSS can help doctors, nurses, and patients make informed decisions by examining patient data from the past, present, and future. Two types of decision support systems are ML systems and rule-based ES. AI systems have shown good accuracy in diagnosing *H. pylori* infections and gastric cancer, while deep learning models identify and classify esophageal squamous cell carcinoma and adenocarcinomas. Natural language processing, or NLP, is being used increasingly in the medical field to give clinicians a way to communicate with patients and to enhance the use of unstructured electronic health records, or EHRs. AI-CDSS implementation in medicine faces challenges due to human factors, social and organizational aspects, and a lack of trust among clinicians. AI technology in healthcare has the potential to become autonomous and threaten human autonomy, but it must be fully under human control and informed about its advantages and risks. Trustworthiness, data security, privacy, and frequent audits are crucial for AI-based diagnostic tools. In the future, patients will be the primary influencers in determining operational performance metrics and product specifications. Cost-effective technologies and advanced decision-support tools can lead to decreased production costs, lead times, and inventories. Collaboration between experts in social and behavioral science, mental health, and cognitive science is recommended for developing ML-based decision-support systems.
