# Chapter 11 AI and big data in post-marketing surveillance

#### Abstract

Post-marketing surveillance, or PMS, involves monitoring a drug’s safety after clinical trials and successful market introduction. The “timely identification” of “novel” adverse drug reactions (ADRs) that differ from one another in terms of their “clinical nature, degree, and frequency” is the main goal of PMS. Examples of new technologies covered in the chapter include machine learning, artificial intelligence, and big data analytics. Many obstacles exist when using AI in post-marketing surveillance, and these technologies have made data privacy protection more difficult. Drug safety monitoring benefits greatly from big data analytics since it makes it easier to identify issues early on and to keep an eye out for potential signals in real time. The creation of adaptable, fact-based quality assurance frameworks that encourage innovation and more informed decision-making can be aided by AI solutions. They can also assist in meeting EU MDR/IVDR regulations for PMS practitioners and manufacturers of medical devices. Moreover, many obstacles are discussed regarding privacy protection when using AI for post-marketing surveillance. Therefore, complicated algorithms are used in healthcare AI applications, which give private AI firms access to patient medical data even when “anonymization” is employed, thus posing a risk to privacy.

Keywords:  surveillance,  data analytics,  drug safety,  machine learning,  adverse drug reaction,  stakeholders,  

## 11.1 Introduction

Within the domain of pharmaceutical sciences, the process of drug innovation is an ongoing endeavor that frequently results in the identification of novel therapeutic agents. A medicine’s development, from the idea to the final product, is thought to be an extremely intricate process that examines every facet of the medication to ensure sufficient safety at the time of approval. Post-marketing surveillance (PMS) can be defined as the process of monitoring a drug’s safety following successful market release from clinical trials \[[1](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_001)\]. The primary objective of PMS research is to identify novel beneficial and detrimental effects. Other important factors include off-label use of drugs, orphan drug concerns, and difficulties in conducting international research studies in the pediatric population. It is a crucial tool that helps paint a clearer picture of the benefits and drawbacks that a drug could start to pose over an extended period of administration by tying the strength of the drug-exposed to the cause of the adverse events \[[2](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_002)\]. Phase IV studies are those related to PMS because most medications cannot be commercialized without first completing clinical trials in phase III. Rare adverse drug reactions (ADRs) will only be partially disclosed by clinical trials conducting safety and efficacy evaluations of novel pharmaceuticals. Results are more realistic because they take place in more natural environments and provide proof to support or improve the safety of authorized medications \[[3](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_003), [4](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_004)\]. Pharmacovigilance and pharmacoepidemiology are the two areas of pharmacology that are covered by post-marketing drug monitoring programs. Pharmacovigilance, also known as medication safety surveillance, has as its main objective the “prompt detection” of “novel” adverse drug reactions (ADRs) that differ from one another in terms of the “clinical nature, severity, as well as frequency.” “Population-based investigation into the use of drugs and the risks associated with these uses” is the definition of pharmacoepidemiology. It is important to stress the importance of pharmacovigilance as well as the idea that a drug’s practical application begins the moment it is marketed \[[5](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_005), [6](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_006), [7](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_007)\].

The World Health Organization suggests that companies look for information in literature and other sources to find out about any new risks associated with using or operating a device. The new Medical Device Regulation (MDR) and the In Vitro Diagnostics Medical Device Regulation (IVDR) from the European Union (EU) demonstrate the substantial increase in PMS demands placed on in vitro diagnostic and medical device companies. Gathering and assessing pertinent specialized, scientific, and technical sources is one requirement \[[8](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_008)\]. The regulations do not specify the use of literature records or the format in which the documentation of the search process should be done; they only call for a thorough description of the chosen data sources, gathering strategies, and evaluation techniques. New EU regulations mandate that companies that produce in vitro diagnostics and medical devices actively search for information regarding the safety and/or efficacy of their products. It will ensure that gadgets remain current and improve early problem detection. The industry will undoubtedly benefit from the shift to proactive surveillance \[[9](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_009), [10](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_010)\]. To comply with the new regulations, businesses will need to invest a significant amount of money in new procedures, staff, and rules. It is noteworthy that many small and medium-sized businesses, particularly those with outdated or fragmented data systems, find it difficult to adequately review and analyze the massive volumes of data offered from various sources. New EU regulations mandate that companies that produce in vitro diagnostics and medical devices actively search for information regarding the safety and/or efficacy of their products \[[11](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_011), [12](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_012)\]. To keep up with the new regulations, businesses will need to invest a significant amount of money in new procedures, staff, and rules. It is noteworthy that many small and medium-sized businesses, particularly those with outdated or fragmented data systems, find it difficult to adequately review and analyze the massive volumes of data available from multiple sources. It is valid across a wide range of sectors, including healthcare \[[13](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_013)\]. As shown in [Fig. 11.1](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_fig_001), artificial intelligence (AI) tools that improve the speed and accuracy of signal detection and enable a wider range of data coverage include machine learning (ML), deep learning (DL), as well as natural language processing (NLP) \[[14](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_014), [15](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_015), [16](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_016), [17](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_017)\]. Numerous aspects of healthcare, such as risk management, medication discovery, diagnosis as well as clinical decision-making, and discovery, are areas where these systems exhibit great promise \[[18](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_018), [19](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_019)\].

![Fig. 11.1: Schematic representation of the analysis of post-marketing surveillance via AI.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-011_fig_001.png)

Fig. 11.1: Schematic representation of the analysis of post-marketing surveillance via AI.

In a study published by Celgene and IBM, deep-learning techniques to evaluate ICSR processing exceeded many accuracy rate thresholds, suggesting the potential of AI to improve data consistency and quality along with reporting efficiency \[[20](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_020)\]. AI in pharmacovigilance is favored by drug safety professionals, per a survey conducted by Celgene’s Global Drug Safety and Risk Management division. This is because it would free them up to concentrate on information evaluation and analysis as opposed to labor-intensive tasks \[[21](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_021), [22](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_022), [23](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_023), [24](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_024)\]. Similarly, artificial intelligence (AI) solutions have a lot to offer PMS practitioners as well as medical device manufacturers. They can accomplish this by lowering the time and cost associated with adhering to EU MDR/IVDR regulations and by establishing flexible, fact-based quality management systems that promote innovation and more deliberate decision-making. Understanding that AI can enhance and simplify adherence to QIAGEN, Huma, and EU MDR/IVDR, AI developed a state-of-the-art post-market intelligence platform to enhance signal detection and expedite PMS workflows linked to risk and performance evaluation through literature surveillance \[[25](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_025)\]. Keeping an eye on published research is essential to PMS because it can offer important insights into pathogen genetics, patient demographics, emerging technologies, the safety and efficacy of devices, and possible off-label use. Nonetheless, more than 35 million citations and abstracts are stored in biomedical databases like PubMed \[[26](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_026)\], and approximately 1.4 million new citations are added annually \[[27](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_027), [28](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_028)\].

## 11.2 Role of machine learning in post-marketing surveillance

There has always been a need for trustworthy, effective, and cost-effective techniques to evaluate students’ written and spoken abilities. Project Essay Grader (PEG), created in 1966 by Ellis Page, is an application that generates computer-generated scores according to writing characteristics like grammar, essay length, and structure. With it, automatic essay scoring (AES) technological advances were introduced \[[29](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_029), [30](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_030)\]. AES systems, like PEG, are designed to make essay scoring faster and easier, with the goal of either supporting or replacing human scorers \[[31](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_031)\]. These kinds of systems use predictive models that analyze the text and, depending on how well the results correlate with scores generated by humans, generate standardized scores at varying degrees of accuracy in forecasting. Since the release of PEG, numerous other AES systems have been invented to improve predictive model accuracy and provide extra features like critical feedback and plagiarism checks. AES systems are currently divided into two main categories: those that use raw-text techniques and those that use hand-engineered essay features \[[32](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_032)\]. Hand-engineered features, which employ domain expert knowledge to produce numerical illustrations of the text that allow basic statistical modeling techniques to find relevant patterns for scoring, have long been the industry standard. In raw text approaches, the space of possible words is mapped to a higher multidimensional integrating space using statistical models, usually neural networks, encoding a deeper comprehension of the word \[[33](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_033)\].

Moreover, BERT turned out to be the most efficient technique for all the macrostructure components. The reason for BERT’s success could be traced back to a method known as transfer learning, which involves pre-training the system on a sizable corpus to give it a deep understanding of the English language. To help explain words with unclear semantics, the technique may be employed as well to train the model to understand each word’s embedding in the wider context of its sequence. Lastly, word embedding creates a semantically connected lower-dimensional space in which unknown phrases can still be portrayed despite their association with known words, which allows BERT and GVEL to handle unseen words in training \[[34](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_034)\]. Alternatively, without any prior training, the CMRF and TIRF methods were compelled to model the narrative structures to score these different elements from the bottom up. For instance, CMRF and TIRF had no structures to deal with the sequential nature of the text, so to score “Initiating Event,” they had to figure out the way an initiating incident could be stated from a small sample of multiple narratives. The inability of these models to handle words that were not included in the initial training set makes them even more limited in practice, and it may have additionally contributed to their subpar results on the tests that were not shown. However, GVEL uses some transfer learning principles and manages sequences naturally through the LSTM. Given the literature’s reports of LSTM performance, it’s plausible that an LSTM with sufficient training data could beat BERT. However, the experiments showed that training an LSTM from unknown weights is very difficult and ultimately not feasible, requiring many narratives to acquire these kinds of dependencies and patterns \[[35](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_035), [36](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_036)\].

While AES systems have many obvious applications, their main use in development has been to score high-stakes written assessments like the SAT and GRE. AES technology is very useful but not always readily available, although well-known systems like PEG, E-Rater, and IntelliMetric are exclusive technologies that are not accessible to the general public. Thus, if clinical practitioners in a variety of disciplines, including psychology, pathology, and education are to benefit from AES technology open-access technology must be developed. The fact that many AES systems rely on holistic scoring techniques, which aim to assess writing quality overall using a point system, is another disadvantage of these systems. Although it is the preferred method for grading standardized exams such as the GRE, scoring systems based on a rubric can provide more detailed feedback by highlighting specific areas of a writer’s work that are strong or weak. Educators, psychologists, and clinicians frequently use tools with a rubric to identify language deficit areas in children as well as track changes in the children’s language abilities over time \[[37](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_037)\]. It is especially recommended that medical professionals, like speech-language pathologists (SLPs), take language samples from their patients to identify their strengths and weaknesses and track improvement during the intervention. During the school-age years, narrative examples of language are the ideal elicitation context; however, due to time constraints, many clinicians do not use them \[[38](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_038)\].

In the medical field, clinical accounts are the primary means of communication. Compared to the generically coded portions of electronic health records (EHRs), narrative notes offer a more comprehensive and distinct account of the patient’s history and assessments, which enhances the context for clinical decision-making. The study of natural language processing, or NLP, is a branch of artificial intelligence that examines the analysis and synthesis of data expressed in natural language. It has repeatedly demonstrated that evidence can be taken out of clinical narratives and made available for large-scale analysis at a later time. The primary justification for machine learning’s popularity as a panacea for the knowledge-gathering information obstacles is that manually annotating data is a simpler task than actually eliciting knowledge \[[39](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_039)\]. However, the amount of data needed to train a machine-learning model might take just as long to annotate as the process of eliciting knowledge \[[40](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_040)\]. Similar to the principle of energy conservation, it appears that the information needed to guide the development of a precise computational model is merely converted from one format to another. Machine learning relies on implicit knowledge in the form of annotations and their distribution, with acquiring moments of these annotations being almost constant, as opposed to explicit understanding in the form of rules. The accessibility of clinical narratives poses a challenge to the machine learning approach due to the sensitive nature of medical data and security concerns \[[41](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_041)\]. Due to these issues (i.e., lack of manually annotated data), the training data may not be representative enough, which could lead to poor performance from the associated ML models \[[42](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_042)\].

As a component of governmental control over the distribution of medical devices (MDs), safety monitoring is crucial both globally \[[43](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_043), [44](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_044), [45](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_045)\] and in Russia \[[46](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_046), [47](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_047)\]. To guarantee the effectiveness and safety of medical devices used daily, the following kind of monitoring is carried out. Greater oversight is necessary when using high-risk (class III) MDs. Therefore, even in the case that there are no risks or adverse events associated with the product, the Board of the Eurasian Economic Commission’s Decision \[[48](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_048)\] mandated that risk class III medical devices be inspected annually for three years following the issuance of a registration certificate. Software as a medical device (SaMD-AI), which is based on machine learning technologies, uses technological solutions to mimic human cognition and produce outcomes that are at least equivalent to human performance \[[49](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_049), [50](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_050)\]. SaMD-AI cannot interpret data, so it needs extra supervision while in use. When applied to a population that differs from that used in machine learning, etc., it could lead to bias \[[51](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_051), [52](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_052)\]. Furthermore, through ongoing (self-)learning, it may integrate deep neural networks, in contrast to SaMD without AI technologies. The expeditious updating of SaMD-AI as an invisible medical device and the ensuing extension of the SaMD-AI re-registration period have raised concerns among researchers. The FDA (Food and Drug Administration) states that a unique registration system covering the entirety of the product life cycle is necessary for the following kind of medical device. The plan needs to be flexible enough to accommodate changes during SaMD-AI adaptation and use, all the while preserving the medical device’s efficacy and safety \[[53](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_053)\]. Other authors go into detail about the procedures that must be followed to guarantee that SaMD-AI is promptly and securely registered and that these products are integrated into actual clinical settings. The authors emphasize that the only way to rule out any potential scatter of SaMD-AI features is to assess SaMD-AI performance using actual clinical data. SaMD-AI’s developers should be aware of any potential risks, such as difficulties when interacting with novel patient populations and inadvertently harmful health outcomes. It is imperative to examine both the predictive indicators, such as positive or negative predictive values, and the SaMD-AI main performance indicators (metrics), including the area under the feature curve \[[52](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_052)\].

## 11.3 Role of big data analytics in post-marketing surveillance

The enormous amount of structured and unstructured data that is produced daily by machines, groups, and people is referred to as “big data.” It includes enormous volumes of data that are difficult to handle or evaluate with conventional data processing methods. Big data is growing exponentially and has several advantages ([Fig. 11.2](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_fig_002)). One major factor contributing to the growth is the introduction of social media, the web, and connected devices. Big data’s velocity is one of its distinguishing features. To gain actionable insights into the unprecedented rate at which data is being generated, organizations must be able to gather, process, and evaluate data in real time. Social media platforms, for example, produce enormous amounts of data from interactions between users, posts, likes, and shares \[[53](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_053)\]. Businesses can make better business decisions and increase customer engagement by utilizing the data to better understand trends, sentiment analysis, and customer preferences. The diversity of big data is another important feature. It includes an extensive range of data types, for example, transaction records, text, photos, videos, and sensor data. Such diversity is beyond the capabilities of conventional databases and information processing methods. However, due to technological developments, such as distributed computing frameworks such as Hadoop and Spark, businesses are now able to store, handle, and analyze sizable and diverse datasets. Organizations can improve decision-making and innovation by integrating and analyzing multiple data sources to find previously hidden correlations, patterns, and insights \[[54](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_054)\].

![Fig. 11.2: Various advantages of big data analytics for post-marketing surveillance.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-011_fig_002.png)

Fig. 11.2: Various advantages of big data analytics for post-marketing surveillance.

A crucial component of the healthcare system, drug safety surveillance is essential to guaranteeing patients’ safety and wellbeing. It entails the methodical gathering, examining, and assessment of information about the risks and side effects connected to the use of drugs. Drug safety surveillance monitors and assesses the safety profiles of pharmaceuticals to identify potential safety concerns, guide regulatory actions, and facilitate the development of risk-reduction and patient safety-improving plans. Data for drug safety surveillance is gathered from a range of sources \[[55](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_055)\]. These sources include unofficial reporting systems, such as the Adverse Event Reporting Systems (AERS) and the Yellow Card Scheme, where patients and healthcare providers can report suspected adverse events. Scientific literature, clinical trials, post-marketing research, and EHRs are additional significant sources of safety information \[[56](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_056)\]. By combining data from multiple sources, drug safety monitoring systems can offer a comprehensive perspective of drug safety, enabling more accurate assessments and timely interventions. The healthcare industry has undergone a revolution due to big data analytics’ capacity to compile vast volumes of health data from numerous sources. Social media platforms, databases of insurance claims, EHRs, and patient forums are some examples of these sources. Combining data from various sources can lead to a thorough understanding of drug usage developments and potential safety issues. The vast amount of medical data that big data analytics can gather presents a unique opportunity to identify and analyze drug use patterns.

Drug safety monitoring benefits greatly from big data analytics since it makes it easier to identify issues early on and to keep an eye out for potential signals in real time. Early warning indicators of adverse events can be recognized and their progression tracked by continuously analyzing data streams gathered from social media posts, online health forums, and spontaneous reporting systems. The capacity of big data analytics to access data from immediate sources is one of its primary advantages \[[57](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_057)\]. User-generated content about drug use on social media and in health forums is abundant and shares actual experiences and viewpoints. By keeping an eye on these platforms, big data analytics techniques can quickly identify conversations, mentions, and sentiments associated with potentially negative events. Real-time monitoring makes it possible to swiftly identify new issues, which enables medical professionals and regulatory agencies to take the necessary action. Using big data analytics, these systems can be continuously monitored to identify trends and patterns in reported adverse events. By sifting through the deluge of reports, algorithms can swiftly spot potential safety signals and provide early warnings of drug-related risks \[[58](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_058), [59](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_059)\]. It is simpler to carry out timely investigations and take the required actions to safeguard patient safety when the capability is present. The ability of big data analytics to capture the evolving nature of drug safety signals is a benefit when it comes to real-time monitoring. Since adverse events can change over time, continuous surveillance is required to effectively monitor them along with any related patterns. Big data analytics methods can continuously analyze data streams to track the development of unfavorable events. They can identify any shifts in the frequency, intensity, or patient demographics of these occurrences due to it. The comprehensive viewpoint makes it possible to approach drug safety proactively, enabling potential issues to be swiftly identified and fixed \[[60](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_060)\].

Another advantage of big data analytics is its ability to integrate multiple data sources, which can aid in validation and prioritization. Integrating information from various sources, including EHRs, clinical trials, and assertion databases, can lead to a more comprehensive and reliable comprehension of the safety indicators. The integration adds to the reliability and importance of the identified signals by bolstering the evidence and validating the findings \[[61](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_061)\]. Integrating information from various sources, including EHRs, clinical trials, and assertion databases, can lead to a more comprehensive and reliable knowledge of safety indicators. This is because these networks most closely resemble big data’s features in everyday pharmacovigilance. Most database networks consist of EMRs and claims databases, but they can also be linked to social media, biological samples kept in biobanks, or genetic information \[[62](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_062), [63](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_063), [64](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_064)\]. Due to record linking, healthcare databases can combine information from several sources to present a complete picture of the medical condition of an individual and facilitate international patient research. This can be done while maintaining patient privacy by using anonymized patient identifiers. Due to the increasing use of healthcare database networks, a growing number of analytical techniques have been employed to leverage the enormous quantities of heterogeneous data \[[65](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_065), [66](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_066), [67](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_067)\]. Not only are dispersed data networks being constructed for analysis, but networks in general are changing. Research collaborations like Observational Health Data Sciences and Informatics (OHDSI) create techniques and resources to make it possible to build the infrastructure of database networks, and data collaborations like the Innovative Medicines Initiative’s PROTECT have demonstrated how multiple database collaborations can be conducted via shared protocols rather than analyzing summary data centrally \[[68](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_068)\].

Big data is becoming more widely used in pharmacovigilance, but it shouldn’t diminish the importance of more conventional methods for observational research. In actuality, most middle-class and low-income nations lack accurate and systematically gathered electronic medical data \[[69](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_069)\]. Until there is a more consistent process of computerizing medical information, observational studies using primary data collection and other methods will continue to be crucial in those countries, along with the use of spontaneous reporting. However, as manipulation and evaluation of data methods proliferate and become more widely available, it is conceivable that the use of big data will only grow in those situations where they are available \[[70](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_070), [71](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_071)\].

## 11.4 Challenges of AI in post-marketing surveillance

When institutions are incentivized to work together to guarantee data protection by design, robust privacy protection can be achieved \[[72](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_072)\]. Privacy can be managed for business healthcare AI applications, but there are new goals at play. It is evident that businesses may not be sufficiently motivated to continuously uphold privacy protection if they can profit from the data or monetize it in some other way and if penalties imposed by law are insufficient to discourage this kind of behavior. There is an additional risk associated with using commercial AI for big data: privacy violations could result from the incredibly intricate algorithmic systems themselves \[[73](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_073), [74](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_074), [75](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_075)\]. The US, Canada, and Europe are just a few of the regions where there has been a rise in healthcare data breaches globally. Furthermore, criminal hackers are making it more difficult to protect medical data, even though they may not be heavily utilizing AI in conjunction with other algorithms just yet. It increases the privacy risks of granting control over patient health data to private AI companies, even when “anonymization” is employed. It is distinct from situations in which state institutions directly manage patient data in that liability, insurance, and other pragmatic considerations are also brought up \[[76](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_076), [77](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_077)\]. Carefully drafted contracts outlining the rights and obligations of the parties involved, as well as responsibility for any potential negative outcomes, will need to be made due to the diverse and complex nature of the legal risks that private AI builders and maintainers may be taking on when interacting with massive amounts of patient data \[[78](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_078), [79](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_079), [80](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_080)\].

The industry is required to adhere to specific requirements set forth by every data protection law, such as the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR), the Health Insurance Portability and Accountability Act (HIPAA), and others. This results in a complex web of international regulatory compliance. Furthermore, because patient privacy is compromised and the pharmaceutical industry has significant intellectual property worth, cybersecurity breaches pose a continuing risk \[[81](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_081)\]. Cyberattacks, like ransomware and information breaches, jeopardize pharmaceutical companies’ reputations and the faith of their patients in addition to their financial risks. Furthermore, the vulnerability is not limited to the corporation; it also exists in the intricate pharmaceutical supply chain, where distributors, manufacturers, and various other stakeholders must work together to protect data privacy. Such a cooperative endeavor entails a fine balance that requires information sharing to progress research while also safeguarding proprietary knowledge. The significance of continuous employee awareness and education efforts is underscored by the fact that human error remains a major contributing factor to data breaches \[[82](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_082)\]. Big data, AI, and machine learning are a few examples of emerging technologies that have increased the difficulty of protecting personal information. The right security measures need to be reevaluated in light of these new challenges. Another factor that increases the importance of privacy protection is the volume of data that is stored, which can range from personal health profiles to the outcomes of clinical trials. The fact that pharmaceutical activities are carried out on a worldwide scale, necessitating strict adherence to numerous data protection laws, makes it more challenging. The situation becomes more complex due to cybersecurity threats. Regular reviews of security precautions are necessary because malevolent actors are always refining their tactics, whether they take the shape of sophisticated hacking attempts or social engineering. Vulnerabilities are introduced at every stage of the drug supply chain, which is crucial to the industry’s operation from manufacturing to distribution \[[83](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_083), [84](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_084)\].

The fact that the pharmaceutical industry is regularly embroiled in court cases and disputes, many of which entail complex analyses of intellectual property laws, makes responding to these challenges even more difficult. Not only can well-known cases impart valuable legal tactics, but they also show us how to carefully balance safeguarding confidential information with fostering a healthy degree of rivalry in the market. Success stories, on the other hand, show how companies are effectively incorporating intellectual property and data privacy into their main business strategies \[[85](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_085)\]. These narratives highlight innovative fixes and industry best practices. Keeping up with the newest emerging trends is one of the hardest things the industry has to do. The emergence of innovations like digital twins and the increasing convergence of data-intensive fields means that the landscape is always changing. Ethical considerations are becoming more and more crucial given how quickly these technologies have been developed and used. Because of this, a proactive approach is needed to foresee and address possible risks. Each type of medical facility supports a distinct data model with a unique set of terminologies and monetary representation, and each type of data model differs from the others. Data from various sources must be merged and formatted to be compatible with the common data model (CDM) across multiple institutions. To do this, engineers and HCPs will have to devote time and effort to data transformation. Because AI-based training models and drug safety algorithms rely on data with annotations, data annotation is crucial for unannotated data. The lack of excellent information for model training and the high annotation cost are obstacles to studies on AI-based drug safety \[[86](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_086)\]. The lack of organized and vetted data available for software creation to detect possible drug safety issues is one of the primary obstacles to AI adoption. Privacy concerns are also raised by the use of AI in pharmacovigilance since participant consent might not be obtained ahead of data being used for other purposes \[[87](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_087)\].

The problem of social gaps is another issue that societies face as a result of the development of artificial intelligence. With every new development, discovery, and invention, people in every country on Earth deal with rising levels of social inequality as well as a dwindling conviction in social justice. Artificial intelligence exacerbates social inequality, despite making it easier to access more information regarding science and technology, international politics, environmental issues, and world events \[[88](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_088)\]. It’s also probable that medical machines and people won’t evolve simultaneously because human feelings are different. Unlike autonomous (robotic) systems, physicians, as well as other health care providers, should consult with or refer colleagues for advice. It is not possible in autonomous systems \[[89](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_089)\]. Robotic physicians and nurses cannot perform the following kind of work. Because robotic nurses and doctors lack human qualities, patients will no longer interact with them with compassion, kindness, or appropriate behavior. Patients won’t be able to behave appropriately either. Additionally, mobile devices are used in the majority of studies on mobility problem detection techniques to gather relevant signals, send them to other devices for analysis, visualize the outcomes, and notify users of the results \[[90](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_090), [91](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_091)\]. Transparency is the capacity to understand how an artificial intelligence system makes decisions. More precisely, the black box’s basic analytical procedures and decision-making process are opaque. Algorithms that are programmed on biased data or that disregard particular demographic traits make these problems worse \[[92](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_092)\].

The sub-issue focuses primarily on the unnecessary or possible harm that AI tools or risky AI in the healthcare industry can cause. Enhancing the quality of care and patient safety, expanding healthcare access, giving patients and doctors local real-time advice, and spotting medical emergencies such as sepsis are just a few advantages of integrating AI into healthcare. However, AI-enabled support services are not error-proof, and their algorithms may suggest dangerous options or advice to patients that could endanger their health \[[93](#9783111573861_015802.xhtml#b_9783111574288-011_ref_093)\].

## 11.5 Conclusion

A crucial procedure in the pharmaceutical sciences, post-marketing surveillance (PMS) keeps an eye on a drug’s safety after it has been put on sale following clinical trials. It assists in identifying adverse and advantageous effects, off-label usage of drugs, orphan drug issues, and difficulties encountered when carrying out global clinical trials on pediatric patients. The Medical Device Regulation (MDR) of the European Union (EU) and the In Vitro Diagnostic Medical Device Regulation (IVDR) have increased PMS requirements for in vitro diagnostic and medical device companies. Compliance with these regulations requires a significant financial investment in new policies, procedures, and skilled staff. Natural language processing, machine learning, and deep learning are examples of artificial intelligence (AI) technologies that can cover a wider range of data and improve signal detection speed and accuracy. Deep-learning methods outperformed the accuracy rate threshold in a Celgene and IBM study, indicating that artificial intelligence (AI) may be able to increase data efficacy, consistency, and reporting efficiency. AI solutions can assist in the development of flexible, evidence-based quality control frameworks that promote creativity and better-informed decision-making. They can also assist in meeting EU MDR/IVDR regulations for PMS practitioners and manufacturers of medical devices. In 1966, automatic essay scoring (AES) technology was first introduced to simplify and expedite essay scoring. It can either supplement or replace human scorers. However, there are a lot of obstacles to privacy protection when using AI for post-marketing surveillance. If businesses can profit through or monetize the data, they might not be inspired to constantly uphold privacy protection, and the existing legal penalties are not enough to deter such behavior. Since healthcare AI applications involve complex algorithms, giving private AI companies access to patient health data presents privacy risks, even in situations where “anonymization” is used. Because of the speed at which these technologies are developing and being used, ethical issues are becoming more important.
