# Chapter 12 Challenges and ethical considerations

#### Abstract

In the healthcare industry, artificial intelligence (AI) is crucial because it helps with disease diagnosis, treatment planning, and operational efficiency. However, there are issues with biases, data breaches, and ethical consequences. The chapter aims to provide some knowledge on the challenges faced by the researchers of AI in healthcare along with some ethical considerations. Collaboration between AI clinicians can succeed if appropriate governance frameworks are in place. An overview of the challenges and ethical quandaries surrounding AI in healthcare is given in the chapter. Explainable AI techniques address issues with bias and transparency. Data privacy, algorithmic transparency, and ethical issues are among the difficulties. Up skilling medical staff, addressing biases, and obtaining patient consent are all necessary before implementing AI in healthcare. Furthermore, healthcare professionals need to believe in AI for it to be adopted. Research suggests that AI holds promise for enhancing patient outcomes and healthcare delivery. More research is necessary to fully grasp how AI will affect healthcare professionals’ roles and responsibilities.

Keywords:  challenges,  ethics,  XAI,  data privacy,  safety,  algorithms,  patient perspective,  patient compliance,  

## 12.1 Introduction

Artificial intelligence (AI) is applied as a system that consists of hardware and software. In terms of software, algorithms are the primary focus of AI. Limited resources, a rise in patient demand, and chronic illness put pressure on healthcare systems. As digital health technologies advance, so does the amount of data in every healthcare facility \[[1](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_001)\]. When appropriately applied, medical professionals could monitor the effectiveness of interventions and preventative measures while concentrating on the causes of illness. Therefore, decision-makers, legislators, and others must be aware of it. Data scientists, computer scientists, along clinical entrepreneurs all agree that AI, particularly machine learning (ML), is important to the success of healthcare reform \[[2](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_002), [3](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_003)\]. In computing, the term “artificial intelligence” describes a computer program’s capacity to carry out operations akin to those of a human intellect, such as learning and reasoning. When it comes to its application, new technology raises concerns as it can result in data breaches and errors. Errors in the high-risk medical domain can yield deadly consequences for the affected patient. It is crucial to recognize that patients engage with doctors during moments of heightened vulnerability in their daily life \[[4](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_004), [5](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_005)\]. Collaborations between AI and clinicians, where AI assists in guiding medical decisions and delivering evidence-based management, can be effective if appropriately utilized (AI-Health). It can provide healthcare services in the domains of diagnosis, medication discovery, epidemiology, personalized care, and operational efficiency. Researchers indicate that a robust governance system is essential to protect individuals from harm, particularly that arising from unethical conduct, when integrating AI solutions into medical practice. The Hippocratic Oath was established due to Hippocrates, the physician, who formulated the ethical foundation of medicine \[[6](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_006)\].

In general, AI in medicine can be divided into three categories: virtual, physical, along hybrid (which combines robots and virtual reality). In both clinical and research contexts, each of these fields has proven to have extraordinary advantages and/or promise \[[7](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_007)\]. A subset of AI known as virtual AI, as well as ML-based AI, consists of a collection of techniques for automatically identifying patterns in data to forecast trends or make decisions in unpredictable circumstances. Machine learning is still being used to help with research in genetics and molecular medicine \[[8](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_008), [9](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_009), [10](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_010), [11](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_011)\]. Furthermore, by combining genotype data from “omics” with phenotypic data from electronic health records (EHRs), AI can support precision medicine. The advent of high-throughput sequencing in recent times has enabled the acquisition of extensive data on proteins, DNA, and molecular interactions. The majority of therapeutic targets and diagnostic biomarkers identified today can be found directly or through interaction with algorithms. These techniques can also be used to optimize research on the creation of novel drugs and treatments, thereby lowering the complexity and expense of these studies. These investigations may encompass data-driven, accurate treatment planning, clinical outcome prediction, and process management simplification (e.g., patient recruitment and retention) \[[12](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_012), [13](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_013), [14](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_014), [15](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_015), [16](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_016)\]. Furthermore, AI has proven to be very beneficial in the digital healthcare era with the extensive use of EHRs and high-throughput sequencing. AI may be able to actively advise doctors when they make clinical judgments. Pathological diagnoses and treatments, imaging, and prediction all show promise and success stories. Robots are the best example of the physical AI branch in healthcare; they help senior patients and support surgeons. Senior care facilities are increasingly using care robots. Sensible Surgeons, a US corporation, manufactures surgical systems that can be operated from a console using a method that is minimally invasive to perform complex surgeries \[[16](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_016), [17](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_017), [18](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_018)\].

AI methods and their applications can be used to diagnose diseases more accurately, care for and monitor the elderly with robots, detect cancer earlier and more quickly than in the past, etc. Massive volumes of data can be processed by ML techniques, which also enable increasingly precise evaluations and forecasts \[[19](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_019), [20](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_020), [21](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_021)\]. While ML has aided in the advancement of AI, it also raises ethical concerns, particularly in the field of healthcare. Prioritizing ethics in the creation and use of AI across industries. The analytical process of evaluating and selecting alternatives in accordance with social, ethical, and legal standards is referred to as ethical decision-making by AI systems \[[22](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_022)\]. The subsequent ethical considerations affect the ongoing development and implementation of AI, especially in the healthcare sector, where technology must adhere to regulations, laws, and privacy norms to safeguard the public interest. Advanced ML algorithms employing deep learning and other complex techniques can generate opaque and challenging-to-interpret models referred to as “black boxes”. The makers of black box models encounter difficulties in elucidating the decision-making processes of AI systems \[[23](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_023), [24](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_024)\]. Users are compelled to make decisions devoid of justification. The issues confronting AI in the domain of chemical medicine can be addressed through multiple strategies. Data augmentation is a technique that entails generating synthetic data to improve existing datasets. As a result, a broader and more varied dataset may be utilized to train ML algorithms, resulting in the generation of more accurate and dependable outcomes. An alternate tactic that seeks to provide clear reasons for the predictions made by machine learning algorithms is the implementation of explainable AI (XAI) tools. It enhances comprehension of the core processes and assumptions that support the predictions, while also alleviating concerns regarding the fairness and bias of AI-based methodologies \[[25](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_025), [26](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_026), [27](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_027), [28](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_028)\].

## 12.2 Challenges in implementing AI in healthcare

To reliably define or predict a variety of tasks, ML as well as deep learning models requires large datasets. Machine learning has advanced most in industries where large datasets are available for backing more complex, precise algorithms. However, data accessibility in the healthcare industry is a complicated problem. Health data is costly for organizations, and sharing it amongst hospitals is viewed with reluctance because each hospital views it as its property, used to manage its patients \[[29](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_029)\]. The data’s continuous availability after the algorithm that analyzes it was introduced is another problem. Ideally, training with ever-larger datasets would necessitate constant improvement for ML-based systems. However, it is frequently challenging to accomplish because of organizational resistance. To achieve it, it has been suggested that for information technology along with AI to make advancements in the healthcare sector, a drastic shift in emphasis from treating specific patients to all-around patient outcomes is required. It will promote data sharing to improve clinical outcomes \[[30](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_030), [31](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_031)\]. Applications using AI create privacy and data security concerns. Security breaches often target sensitive health data. Protecting patient data is therefore essential. People may begin to mistake AI systems for humans as AI develops, which would increase worries about data privacy and allow for more inadvertent data collection. Patient consent is crucial for addressing data privacy issues, as without sufficient individual consent, healthcare organizations may permit extensive utilization of patient data for AI training \[[32](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_032), [33](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_033)\].

Extensive datasets are a fundamental element of AI-driven systems and technologies, prompting privacy concerns related to data sharing and gathering. Due to the inclusion of personal information in patient records, the interchange and management of illness-related data across several databases is exceedingly challenging \[[34](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_034), [35](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_035)\]. It indicates that software development organizations must adhere to secrecy regulations, potentially obstructing AI development. AI technology relies on machine learning to make conclusions based on available information, rather than taking into account the unique circumstances of each patient, hence raising moral, ethical, and legal concerns. AI technology should conform to the moral standards, legal obligations, and personal convictions that govern individuals’ conduct in public areas. Numerous industries have transitioned from isolated silos in the digital era. It is also applicable to the medical domain \[[36](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_036)\]. The healthcare sector was previously perceived as involving medical professionals, including doctors and nurses, providing patient care. In addition to access to superior medical treatment, contemporary health is also achieved via the adoption of healthy lifestyle choices, including a balanced diet, regular exercise, and daily self-care practices. It has rendered preventive medicine increasingly vital, complicating the differentiation between the roles of a physician, the utilization of technology, and the maintenance of a healthy lifestyle. AI is not limited by temporal or spatial parameters. Consequently, the concept of a closed healthcare system has become obsolete \[[37](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_037)\].

The advancement of AI technology presents the next obstacle. Over fitting can happen when the system picks up on irrelevant correlations between patient characteristics and results. The algorithm predicts using unsuitable features as a result of having excessive variable parameters about outcomes \[[38](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_038)\]. As a result, the algorithm may work correctly when applied to the training data set, but it may yield unreliable results when applied to future events. The leakage of data is another issue. The algorithm’s usefulness for predicting outcomes beyond the training dataset may be questioned if a covariate in the dataset unintentionally revealed the result, as would occur if the algorithm’s optimistic accuracy was too high \[[39](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_039), [40](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_040)\]. One prevalent criticism leveled at AI is the “black-box” problem. It is often difficult for deep learning algorithms to provide comprehensive explanations for the predictions they make. The inability of the system to safeguard its conclusions creates a legal challenge if the recommendations turn out to be incorrect. It also makes it more difficult for scientists to understand how the data and the predictions relate to one another. More significantly, patients may become less trusting of the system as a result of the “black box” \[[41](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_041), [42](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_042)\].

Since the advent of AI, it has been accompanied with objections and ethical dilemmas. In addition to the previously addressed concerns around data security and confidentiality, accountability remains the primary apprehension. In the healthcare sector, poor decisions can yield significant consequences, and there is a consensus that accountability must be established. Occasionally, individuals perceive AI as a “black box,” rendering the underlying algorithmic logic of a particular advice or prediction inscrutable \[[43](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_043)\]. It can be contended that algorithms employed in lower-risk applications, which emphasize efficiency or enhanced operations rather than patient-centeredness, should not be apprehensive about the “black-box” problem. Therefore, if the system is defective, it remains ambiguous who should assume responsibility. Attributing responsibility to the doctor may appear unjust, as they lacked involvement or authority in the algorithm’s creation; meanwhile, assigning accountability to the developer looks overly disconnected from the clinical environment. AI cannot legally make any healthcare decisions in China; all decisions must involve human decision-making and accountability \[[44](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_044)\].

A primary concern with AI in healthcare is safety, as illustrated in [Fig. 12.1](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_fig_001). IBM Watson for Oncology exemplifies the application of AI algorithms to analyze medical record data and support physicians in providing cancer therapy alternatives to patients. Recently, it has encountered fire for purportedly endorsing “unsafe and incorrect” cancer therapies \[[45](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_045)\].

![Fig. 12.1: Schematic representation of challenges and potential solutions of AI in healthcare.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-012_fig_001.png)

Fig. 12.1: Schematic representation of challenges and potential solutions of AI in healthcare.

Moreover, upholding a requisite level of transparency is crucial for safeguarding patient trust and security. In an ideal scenario, all data and algorithms would be accessible for public scrutiny; nonetheless, there are valid concerns around the protection of investments and intellectual property, as well as the mitigation of cyber security risks \[[46](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_046)\]. The healthcare sector encounters significant ethical and privacy challenges that require strict compliance with laws, norms, and regulations. The implementation of AI in medicine raises several ethical and privacy concerns, including the confidentiality of patient information, the moral implications of innovation, and the tangible effects of technical advancements on both physicians and patients. To effectively leverage AI in healthcare, strategic decisions must be taken that balance competing interests and values, including privacy, reliability, intellectual property rights, and openness \[[47](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_047), [48](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_048), [49](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_049), [50](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_050), [51](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_051)\]. Establishing patient trust is essential in the healthcare industry. It is especially applicable to technology based on AI. Healthcare experts and decision-makers acknowledge that, due to the historical significance of in-person consultations with physicians, the public may be reluctant to depend exclusively on AI-generated advice. Moreover, healthcare personnel may have difficulties in utilizing these technologies due to the public’s pervasive misconceptions regarding the potential benefits and limitations of AI in medicine \[[52](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_052), [53](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_053), [54](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_054)\].

Moreover, certain healthcare professionals such as physicians, nurses, and nursing assistants may be inadequately attending to digital technology, thereby obstructing the implementation of AI in healthcare. The interpretability and opacity of AI algorithms pose significant challenges, particularly in the context of unstructured data. AI systems encounter difficulties as hospital administrators and physicians often grapple with technology apprehensions. Interoperability standards are essential for employing AI models and enabling the integration of data from various electronic health records \[[55](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_055), [56](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_056)\].

## 12.3 Ethical consideration in AI-driven healthcare

To ensure the responsible and beneficial deployment XAI in healthcare, it is essential to rigorously assess its ethical ramifications. Researchers emphasize the significance of AI efficacy, interpretability, and explicability in health technologies. Their findings indicate that organizations assessing healthcare technology must acquire substantial knowledge regarding the functionality of machine learning algorithms and their predictive capabilities \[[57](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_057)\]. The authors advocate for the development and application of tools to assess these factors to foster a sense of accountability among stakeholders. Certain researchers employ AI for diagnostic purposes and examine the potential benefits and drawbacks associated with the interpretability and reliability of AI. While AI has the capacity to enhance diagnostic output and accuracy, it poses a risk to the skill sets of the clinical profession. The research advocates for the utilization of AI as an adjunct to human expertise instead of attempting to supplant it. Potential biases in AI and big data applications utilized in the healthcare sector have been highlighted by certain sources \[[58](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_058), [59](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_059)\]. They offer recommendations for improving the equity of AI algorithms and caution against training these algorithms with non-representative samples. The authors contribute to the policy discussion on data oversight in the AI era by highlighting the necessity of incentivizing patients to disclose their health data. The authors contend that the revenue-generating nature of digital copyright content, such as music, can impart important knowledge and provide a GDPR-based remedy that leaves data flow unhindered \[[60](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_060)\]. They contend that the strategy may provide a workable and proven way to encourage data sharing without restricting access to it, which is essential for the advancement and efficacy of healthcare AI. Academics talk about how urgently human-centered AI is needed in healthcare, especially in the so-called Global South a region mostly made up of low-resource areas \[[61](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_061), [62](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_062)\]. According to Okolo, biased AI systems may inadvertently widen the gaps that currently exist in these fields, so their use should be avoided. The author emphasizes how crucial it is to consider local requirements and conditions when creating AI systems to provide more equitable and effective healthcare solutions. Similarly, researchers discuss the moral implications of integrating AI and big data into mental health services a notion the author refers to as “iHealth.” In his thorough ethical examination of the possibilities of iHealth, Rubeis identifies three double-edged swords: data mining, ecological momentary assessment (EMA), and self-monitoring \[[63](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_063)\].

Moreover, transparency should be given top priority, and XAI should give clear justifications for its judgments as shown in [Fig. 12.2](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_fig_002). It promotes accountability by enabling consumers and medical professionals to comprehend the reasoning behind AI-generated recommendations or diagnoses. Transparent AI systems help consumers validate as well as trust the results by avoiding the “black box” problem. Preexisting biases may be reinforced and amplified by AI systems that were trained on biased data, which might have discriminatory impacts on healthcare outcomes. When implementing XAI ethically, biases must be actively and thoughtfully taken into account during the phases of data collection, preliminary processing, and model training \[[64](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_064)\]. XAI system bias monitoring and remediation can lead to more reasonable healthcare practices. Patients have a right to know if AI, including XAI, is used in their treatment. To gain informed consent, it is imperative to have clear communication about the purpose, constraints, and possible hazards associated with AI systems. It should be possible for patients to obtain human explanations and guidance, guaranteeing that their choices and autonomy are honored. Important elements of ethical XAI include putting patient welfare first and making sure AI systems make choices that are in the best interests of the patient \[[65](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_065)\]. When making decisions, healthcare professionals should be assisted by XAI, which should be built with their specific needs and preferences in mind. Healthcare professionals must comprehend and apply XAI systems correctly. It entails upholding their clinical expertise, critically assessing AI outputs, and being aware of the constraints and potential biases of AI systems. Healthcare workers should receive sufficient XAI education and training to guarantee its responsible and efficient use. XAI development and application in healthcare can be guided by adhering to accepted ethical frameworks, including the principles of kindness, nonmaleficence, independence, and justice. Healthcare-specific ethical guidelines, like those offered by governmental or professional medical organizations can help navigate the particular ethical challenges that XAI presents \[[66](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_066), [67](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_067)\].

![Fig. 12.2: Schematic representation of ethical considerations of AI in healthcare.](graphic/converted/b_9783111574288-012_fig_002.png)

Fig. 12.2: Schematic representation of ethical considerations of AI in healthcare.

Another researcher explains that privacy is a key ethical factor to consider when using AI. Concerns about how AI systems gather, store, and use personal data are known as privacy issues in AI. AI systems can gather and analyze substantial amounts of personal data, and improper management of the data may lead to significant privacy concerns \[[68](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_068)\]. AI systems often collect substantial amounts of personal data from individuals, which are subsequently utilized to make determinations about them. It prompts enquiries on the methodologies employed for data collection, storage, and utilization. The implementation of AI in surveillance systems may infringe upon privacy rights. Four principal ethical issues must be addressed for AI in healthcare to achieve its maximum potential: (1) informed permission for data utilization; (2) safety and integrity; (3) fairness in algorithms alongside biases; and (4) protection of personal data privacy. The majority of legal discourse on AI has been on the constraints of algorithmic transparency. The application of AI in high-risk situations has necessitated transparent, equitable, and accountable AI design and governance. Accessibility and comprehensibility of information are the two essential elements of transparency. It is standard practice to deliberately obscure the mechanisms of algorithmic operations \[[69](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_069), [70](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_070)\]. The potential for bias in AI algorithms, which may lead to disparities in healthcare results, represents another ethical problem. An unrepresentative training dataset can result in biased AI models that disproportionately affect specific demographic groups. It may result in inequitable access to healthcare and intensify pre-existing disparities in medical outcomes. To ensure fairness and equity, developers must actively combat bias through inclusive and diverse data sets, constant monitoring, and iterative algorithm improvement \[[71](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_071)\]. An important ethical consideration in AI decision-making is interpretability, particularly in the medical field where decisions affect patients’ quality of life. Deep learning networks and other complex AI models can be hard to interpret, resulting in it being hard to understand the way the system comes to a particular conclusion \[[72](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_072)\].

Moreover, another ethical cornerstone in AI applications for healthcare that needs particular consideration is informed consent. Patients ought to be made aware of how AI technologies are being used in their medical treatment and given the choice to accept or reject AI-driven diagnoses or treatments. It entails outlining the advantages of AI in healthcare as well as their drawbacks and possible hazards \[[73](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_073)\]. AI’s ability to automate some tasks in the healthcare industry could result in job displacement, which raises ethical questions about how it will affect the workforce. Maintaining human roles while taking advantage of AI’s efficiency gains requires careful consideration. Prioritizing reskilling and up skilling initiatives are necessary for healthcare organizations to guarantee that their workforce is capable of adjusting to and cooperating with AI technologies \[[74](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_074), [75](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_075)\].

## 12.4 Impact on healthcare professionals

AI has applications in healthcare that include task automation, anomaly detection, correlation analysis, unstructured data analysis, and human worker support. These are accomplished by using DL, ML, and NLP algorithms. NLP algorithms identify key terms and phrases in unprocessed electronic medical record data to assist with decision-making. The way AI can transform data into outcomes that could enhance clinical practices and the caliber of care given by medical professionals in innovative ways is fascinating to watch \[[76](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_076), [77](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_077), [78](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_078), [79](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_079), [80](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_080)\]. Research has examined whether or not AI will be accepted or used, depending on how beneficial the technology is viewed to be and how to make it work better. Research on AI has mostly concentrated on the technology – where to connect it and how to best utilize it. Nonetheless, the human being is the other essential element in all of it. It is now necessary to take into account how the technology will affect the user, the healthcare professional – and how it will influence their capacity to provide care \[[81](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_081), [82](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_082), [83](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_083)\]. The use and capability of AI will vary amongst healthcare professionals and disciplines. Since AI in health has no widely accepted definition, healthcare professionals might not be aware that they are utilizing AI in their work. It is crucial to comprehend how clinicians view AI in healthcare delivery to guarantee a long-term healthcare workforce prepared to manage its quickly evolving digital healthcare landscape. More research is required to determine the interactions between clinicians and technology \[[84](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_084), [85](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_085), [86](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_086)\]. Researchers investigating the underlying factors influencing healthcare professionals’ endorsement of an AIMDSS met the criteria. Medical practitioners are less inclined to utilize AI in their routine practice when they harbor skepticism about the technology or lack awareness of its capacity to enhance patient outcomes or care delivery, as indicated by the researcher’s limited observational study. The conclusion pertains specifically to the healthcare sector, highlighting similarities in aspects of digital health technology that do not incorporate AI \[[87](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_087), [88](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_088), [89](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_089), [90](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_090)\].

Researchers investigated how patients and healthcare providers interacted with digital technology used in the delivery of healthcare. The findings demonstrated that healthcare professionals were less likely to adopt digital technology if they did not know what it was used for or did not think it would enhance patient-provider relationships or the standard of care. Prior studies have indicated that healthcare practitioners possess a restricted comprehension of pertinent digital systems; consequently, a more profound understanding of the benefits and drawbacks of these systems is imperative, especially regarding their application in the healthcare sector \[[91](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_091), [92](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_092)\]. Researchers looked at the use of telemedicine in the treatment of diabetic foot ulcer patients and concluded that the context of technology use will be informed by the experiences of medical professionals. Others claimed that medical technology will have an impact on how doctors perceive different options for therapy and treatment, as well as how they comprehend patients’ illnesses and interpret their complaints. Consequently, more study is required to determine how AI will affect the job and responsibilities of healthcare professionals \[[93](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_093), [94](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_094), [95](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_095)\]. Medical professionals seemed to be optimistic about the benefits AI tools could provide for their patients. According to a survey conducted among healthcare professionals, AI tools created by industrialists have the potential to save doctors’ time, perform vigilant and watchful tasks, enhance population monitoring, mitigate certain issues associated with medical deserts, and even enhance management challenges within the healthcare industry, particularly in hospitals. Therefore, AI may be a way to usher in a more effective era of medicine, enhancing patient safety while lowering costs and improving care \[[96](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_096), [97](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_097)\]. Thus, AI would signify a revolution in treating patients and medical practices for certain doctors, but it would also signify a continuation of the field’s ongoing developments in medicine for other doctors. Nonetheless, every AI tool scenario discussed in the interviews was based on ongoing research. Healthcare experts then informed the interviewees that AI is still in its early stages; pointing out that only a few of these initiatives have yet shown real-world success. Physicians also noted a discrepancy between scientific findings and the sensationalized media announcements \[[98](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_098)\].

For the concerned healthcare professionals, doctors’ primary interest in AI is not just a wishful dream but also a result of the overwhelming volume of data that makes it more difficult for doctors to make diagnoses in the modern world. They thus stated that to evaluate and categorize such data, they require tools. Furthermore, rather than relying solely on AI tools, some doctors think that they ought to first inquire about themselves and what the needs and potential changes are. Physicians expressed their lack of readiness to face criminal charges if an AI tool is found to have made a medical error, should these tools become increasingly important in medical decision-making \[[99](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_099), [100](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_100)\].

## 12.5 Patient perspective and engagement

It is well-known how the public feels about sharing health data for research purposes \[[101](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_101), [102](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_102), [103](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_103)\]. While worries about data sharing’s security, confidentiality, and commercial implications are often raised, people generally favor the research when they believe that it will benefit society or the public at large and when they have faith in the institutions carrying it out \[[104](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_104)\]. Nonetheless, the perceptions of the public and patients on the dissemination of medical information for AI research are inadequately articulated \[[105](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_105)\]. Conditional public support for data sharing cannot be presumed to apply to the subject of study, as data sharing for AI research is a contentious issue. The medical literature in the field is rapidly expanding, and AI has the capacity to significantly transform patient interactions with healthcare practitioners. A recent journal issue analyzed the merits and drawbacks of AI technology, including enhancements in healthcare efficacy and quality, as well as threats to patient autonomy, informed consent, and privacy. However, there is a lack of depth and clarity in the present knowledge of how patients view AI and its use in healthcare \[[106](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_106)\]. According to other researchers, people will likely lose faith in AI systems’ abilities. People might not trust AI’s diagnostic and predictive capabilities, for example, when it comes to medical interventions. According to a different study, users’ perceptions of AI systems’ trustworthiness are influenced by their autonomy \[[107](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_107), [108](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_108), [109](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_109)\]. Furthermore, fewer participants signed up for an AI diagnosis in a survey some researchers conducted to learn about consumer views of AI in medicine than for a diagnosis from a healthcare provider. The majority of respondents in the same study preferred a human provider over AI, even if there was a greater chance of a misdiagnosis. Additionally, they showed that patients are more inclined to select AI if the doctor makes the final treatment decision rather than just the AI \[[110](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_110)\].

Historically, medical practitioners predominantly determined the health decisions of their patients. It constrains the opportunities for collaboration and collective decision-making between patients and healthcare providers. Studies have shown that digital technology has transformed the relationship between patients and medical personnel \[[111](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_111), [112](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_112), [113](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_113), [114](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_114)\]. The implementation of these technologies has fostered more collaborative ties between patients and doctors, enabling people to more readily express their concerns over their care. Patients with more awareness of their condition are those that commence self-management. It is characterized as the systematic distribution of knowledge and supportive measures by healthcare professionals to improve individuals’ abilities and confidence in managing their health issues \[[115](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_115)\]. In an ideal scenario, patients would possess the confidence to address their social, emotional, and physical challenges. Digital technology is enhancing patient engagement and preventive medicine within the healthcare sector. Research indicates that the incorporation of digital technology in healthcare enhances patients’ awareness of their lifestyle choices and encourages them to engage more proactively in their health management. Researchers found that veterans exhibited increased motivation to manage their health when they had access to their health data. Moreover, personalization is modifying a digital platform, product, or service’s user experience to align with an individual’s specific needs, interests, behaviors, and characteristics \[[116](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_116), [117](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_117), [118](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_118)\]. It entails leveraging data and algorithms to produce interactions, recommendations, and user-specific content that are pertinent and helpful. It facilitates the change in the power dynamic from physicians making decisions for the sake of their patients to a collaborative process in which the needs of the patients are taken into account. Developing a user-friendly online interface for patients may be essential to boosting their engagement with their care \[[119](#9783111573861_017182.xhtml#b_9783111574288-012_ref_119)\].

## 12.6 Conclusion

AI is a potent instrument in the field of healthcare, especially when it comes to ML, which helps to monitor the efficacy of interventions and understand illnesses. Healthcare organizations may be resistant to implementing AI due to issues with data accessibility and organizational resistance, so a shift toward overall patient outcomes is required. For data privacy, patient consent is essential, particularly since AI relies heavily on massive datasets. Responsible AI implementation in healthcare requires ethical considerations that prioritize patient trust, accountability, and transparency. Healthcare outcomes trained on biased data by AI systems can be discriminatory, highlighting the need to address biases in data collection as well as model training. Limitations on algorithmic transparency have been the focus of legal discussions; however, in high-risk scenarios, transparent, equitable, and ethical AI design is essential. Creating reskilling programs as a priority is essential to ensuring that medical professionals can use AI technologies. The need for more education on the benefits and drawbacks of AI is highlighted by healthcare professionals’ limited grasp of digital systems. Although worries about criminal penalties for AI errors remain, healthcare professionals are becoming more and more interested in using AI to handle the massive amount of data needed for diagnosis. Ultimately, AI in healthcare possesses the capacity to improve patient outcomes and the delivery of care; yet, factors such as transparency, data privacy, and ethical concerns must be addressed. The successful use of AI necessitates collaboration among medical experts. Further research is necessary to comprehensively understand the impact of AI on patients and healthcare professionals for the appropriate and effective integration of AI in the healthcare sector.
